<p><b>Nepal</b> (<span class="rt-commentedText nowrap"><small>English: </small><span class="IPA nopopups noexcerpt" lang="en-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/English" title="Help:IPA/English">/<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="&#39;n&#39; in &#39;nigh&#39;">n</span><span title="/ɪ/: &#39;i&#39; in &#39;kit&#39;">ɪ</span><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="&#39;p&#39; in &#39;pie&#39;">p</span><span title="/ɔː/: &#39;au&#39; in &#39;fraud&#39;">ɔː</span><span title="&#39;l&#39; in &#39;lie&#39;">l</span></span>/</a></span></span>;<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Nepali_language" title="Nepali language">Nepali</a>: <span lang="ne"><a href="https://ne.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E0%A4%A8%E0%A5%87%E0%A4%AA%E0%A4%BE%E0%A4%B2" class="extiw" title="ne:नेपाल">नेपाल</a></span> <small></small><span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="ne-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/Nepali" title="Help:IPA/Nepali">[nepal]</a></span>), formally the <b>Federal Democratic Republic of Nepal</b> (<a href="/wiki/Nepali_language" title="Nepali language">Nepali</a>: <span lang="ne">सङ्घीय लोकतान्त्रिक गणतन्त्र नेपाल</span>),<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> is a <a href="/wiki/Landlocked_country" title="Landlocked country">landlocked country</a> in <a href="/wiki/South_Asia" title="South Asia">South Asia</a>. It is mainly situated in the <a href="/wiki/Himalayas" title="Himalayas">Himalayas</a>, but also includes parts of the <a href="/wiki/Indo-Gangetic_Plain" title="Indo-Gangetic Plain">Indo-Gangetic Plain</a>, bordering the <a href="/wiki/Tibet_Autonomous_Region" title="Tibet Autonomous Region">Tibet Autonomous Region</a> of China <a href="/wiki/China%E2%80%93Nepal_border" title="China–Nepal border">to the north</a>, and India <a href="/wiki/India%E2%80%93Nepal_border" title="India–Nepal border">in the south, east, and west</a>, while it is narrowly separated from <a href="/wiki/Bangladesh" title="Bangladesh">Bangladesh</a> by the <a href="/wiki/Siliguri_Corridor" title="Siliguri Corridor">Siliguri Corridor</a>, and from <a href="/wiki/Bhutan" title="Bhutan">Bhutan</a> by the <a href="/wiki/States_and_union_territories_of_India" title="States and union territories of India">Indian state</a> of <a href="/wiki/Sikkim" title="Sikkim">Sikkim</a>. Nepal has a <a href="/wiki/Geography_of_Nepal" title="Geography of Nepal">diverse geography</a>, including <a href="/wiki/Terai" title="Terai">fertile plains</a>, subalpine forested hills, and eight of the world's ten <a href="/wiki/List_of_highest_mountains#List" class="mw-redirect" title="List of highest mountains">tallest mountains</a>, including <a href="/wiki/Mount_Everest" title="Mount Everest">Mount Everest</a>, the highest point on Earth. Nepal is a multi-ethnic, multi-lingual, multi-religious and multi-cultural state, with <a href="/wiki/Nepali_language" title="Nepali language">Nepali</a> as the official language. <a href="/wiki/Kathmandu" title="Kathmandu">Kathmandu</a> is the nation's capital and the <a href="/wiki/List_of_cities_in_Nepal" title="List of cities in Nepal">largest city</a>.
</p><p>The name "Nepal" is first recorded in texts from the <a href="/wiki/Vedic_period" title="Vedic period">Vedic period</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Indian_subcontinent" title="Indian subcontinent">Indian subcontinent</a>, the era in <a href="/wiki/Ancient_Nepal" class="mw-redirect" title="Ancient Nepal">ancient Nepal</a> when <a href="/wiki/Hinduism" title="Hinduism">Hinduism</a> was founded, the predominant religion of the country. In the middle of the first millennium BC, <a href="/wiki/Gautama_Buddha" class="mw-redirect" title="Gautama Buddha">Gautama Buddha</a>, the founder of <a href="/wiki/Buddhism" title="Buddhism">Buddhism</a>, was born in <a href="/wiki/Lumbini" title="Lumbini">Lumbini</a> in southern Nepal. Parts of northern Nepal were intertwined with the culture of <a href="/wiki/Tibet" title="Tibet">Tibet</a>. The centrally located <a href="/wiki/Kathmandu_Valley" title="Kathmandu Valley">Kathmandu Valley</a> is intertwined with the culture of <a href="/wiki/Indo-Aryan_people" class="mw-redirect" title="Indo-Aryan people">Indo-Aryans</a>, and was the seat of the prosperous <a href="/wiki/Newar_people" title="Newar people">Newar</a> confederacy known as <a href="/wiki/Nepal_Mandala" title="Nepal Mandala">Nepal Mandala</a>. The Himalayan branch of the ancient <a href="/wiki/Silk_Road" title="Silk Road">Silk Road</a> was dominated by the <a href="/wiki/Lhasa_Newar" title="Lhasa Newar">valley's traders</a>. The cosmopolitan region developed distinct traditional <a href="/wiki/Newa_art" class="mw-redirect" title="Newa art">art</a> and <a href="/wiki/Architecture_of_Nepal" title="Architecture of Nepal">architecture</a>. By the 18th century, the <a href="/wiki/Gorkha_Kingdom" title="Gorkha Kingdom">Gorkha Kingdom</a> achieved the <a href="/wiki/Unification_of_Nepal" title="Unification of Nepal">unification of Nepal</a>. The <a href="/wiki/Shah_dynasty" title="Shah dynasty">Shah dynasty</a> established the <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Nepal" title="Kingdom of Nepal">Kingdom of Nepal</a> and later formed an alliance with the <a href="/wiki/British_Empire" title="British Empire">British Empire</a>, under its <a href="/wiki/Rana_dynasty" title="Rana dynasty">Rana dynasty</a> of <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Nepal" title="Prime Minister of Nepal">premiers</a>. The country was never colonised but served as a buffer state between <a href="/wiki/Qing_dynasty" title="Qing dynasty">Imperial China</a> and <a href="/wiki/British_India" class="mw-redirect" title="British India">British India</a>. <a href="/wiki/Parliamentary_democracy" class="mw-redirect" title="Parliamentary democracy">Parliamentary democracy</a> was introduced in 1951 but was twice suspended by Nepalese monarchs, in 1960 and 2005. The <a href="/wiki/Nepalese_Civil_War" title="Nepalese Civil War">Nepalese Civil War</a> in the 1990s and early 2000s resulted in the establishment of a <a href="/wiki/Secular" class="mw-redirect" title="Secular">secular</a> <a href="/wiki/Republic" title="Republic">republic</a> in 2008, ending the world's last <a href="/wiki/Hinduism" title="Hinduism">Hindu</a> monarchy.
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Constitution_of_Nepal" title="Constitution of Nepal">Constitution of Nepal</a>, adopted in 2015, affirms the country as a <a href="/wiki/Secular_state" title="Secular state">secular</a> <a href="/wiki/Federal_parliamentary_republic" title="Federal parliamentary republic">federal parliamentary republic</a> divided into <a href="/wiki/Provinces_of_Nepal" title="Provinces of Nepal">seven provinces</a>. Nepal was admitted to the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations" title="United Nations">United Nations</a> in 1955, and friendship treaties were signed with India in 1950 and China in 1960. Nepal hosts the permanent secretariat of the <a href="/wiki/South_Asian_Association_for_Regional_Cooperation" title="South Asian Association for Regional Cooperation">South Asian Association for Regional Cooperation</a> (SAARC), of which it is a founding member. Nepal is also a member of the <a href="/wiki/Non-Aligned_Movement" title="Non-Aligned Movement">Non-Aligned Movement</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Bay_of_Bengal_Initiative" class="mw-redirect" title="Bay of Bengal Initiative">Bay of Bengal Initiative</a>. The <a href="/wiki/Nepalese_Armed_Forces" title="Nepalese Armed Forces">Nepalese Armed Forces</a> are the fifth-largest in South Asia; and are notable for their <a href="/wiki/Gurkha" title="Gurkha">Gurkha</a> history, particularly during the <a href="/wiki/World_war" title="World war">world wars</a>, and has been a significant contributor to <a href="/wiki/United_Nations_peacekeeping" title="United Nations peacekeeping">United Nations peacekeeping</a> operations.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Etymology">Etymology</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Name_of_Nepal" title="Name of Nepal">Name of Nepal</a></div>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:N%C4%93p%C4%81la_in_the_Allahabad_Pillar_inscription.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4a/N%C4%93p%C4%81la_in_the_Allahabad_Pillar_inscription.jpg/220px-N%C4%93p%C4%81la_in_the_Allahabad_Pillar_inscription.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="90" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4a/N%C4%93p%C4%81la_in_the_Allahabad_Pillar_inscription.jpg/330px-N%C4%93p%C4%81la_in_the_Allahabad_Pillar_inscription.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4a/N%C4%93p%C4%81la_in_the_Allahabad_Pillar_inscription.jpg/440px-N%C4%93p%C4%81la_in_the_Allahabad_Pillar_inscription.jpg 2x" data-file-width="594" data-file-height="244" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:N%C4%93p%C4%81la_in_the_Allahabad_Pillar_inscription.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>"Nēpāla" in the late <a href="/wiki/Brahmi_script" title="Brahmi script">Brahmi script</a>, in the <a href="/wiki/Allahabad_Pillar_inscription" class="mw-redirect" title="Allahabad Pillar inscription">Allahabad Pillar inscription</a> of <a href="/wiki/Samudragupta" title="Samudragupta">Samudragupta</a> (350-375 CE).<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup></div></div></div>
<p>Before the <a href="/wiki/Unification_of_Nepal" title="Unification of Nepal">unification of Nepal</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Kathmandu_Valley" title="Kathmandu Valley">Kathmandu Valley</a> was known as <i>Nepal</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;a&#93;</a></sup> The precise origin of the term <i>Nepāl</i> is uncertain. <i>Nepal</i> appears in <a href="/wiki/Ancient_Indian" class="mw-redirect" title="Ancient Indian">ancient Indian</a> literary texts dated as far back as the fourth century BC.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Avoid_weasel_words" class="mw-redirect" title="Wikipedia:Avoid weasel words"><span title="The material near this tag possibly uses too vague attribution or weasel words. (May 2022)">which?</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> An absolute chronology can not be established, as even the oldest texts may contain anonymous contributions dating as late as the <a href="/wiki/Early_modern_period" title="Early modern period">early modern period</a>. Academic attempts to provide a plausible theory are hindered by the lack of a complete picture of history and insufficient understanding of linguistics or relevant Indo-European and Tibeto-Burman languages.<sup id="cite_ref-Malla_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Malla-19">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to <a href="/wiki/Hindu_mythology" title="Hindu mythology">Hindu mythology</a>, Nepal derives its name from an ancient Hindu sage called <i>Ne</i>, referred to variously as <i>Ne Muni</i> or <i>Nemi</i>. According to <i>Pashupati Purāna</i>, as a place protected by <i>Ne</i>, the country in the heart of the Himalayas came to be known as <i>Nepāl</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;b&#93;</a></sup> According to <i>Nepāl Mahātmya</i>,<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;c&#93;</a></sup> <i>Nemi</i> was charged with protection of the country by <a href="/wiki/Pashupati" title="Pashupati">Pashupati</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Prasad_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Prasad-22">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> According to <a href="/wiki/Buddhist_mythology" title="Buddhist mythology">Buddhist mythology</a>, <a href="/wiki/Manjushri" title="Manjushri">Manjushri</a> <a href="/wiki/Bodhisattva" title="Bodhisattva">Bodhisattva</a> drained a primordial lake of <a href="/wiki/N%C4%81ga" title="Nāga">serpents</a> to create the Nepal valley and proclaimed that <i><a href="/wiki/Adi-Buddha" title="Adi-Buddha">Adi-Buddha</a></i> <i>Ne</i> would take care of the community that would settle it. As the cherished of <i>Ne</i>, the valley would be called <i>Nepāl</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> According to <i>Gopalarājvamshāvali</i>, the <a href="/wiki/Genealogy" title="Genealogy">genealogy</a> of ancient <a href="/wiki/Gopal_Bansa" class="mw-redirect" title="Gopal Bansa">Gopala dynasty</a> compiled <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1380s</span>, Nepal is named after <i>Nepa</i> the cowherd, the founder of the Nepali scion of the <a href="/wiki/Abhira_tribe" title="Abhira tribe">Abhiras</a>. In this account, the cow that issued milk to the spot, at which <i>Nepa</i> discovered the <i><a href="/wiki/Jyotirlinga" title="Jyotirlinga">Jyotirlinga</a></i> of <a href="/wiki/Pashupatinath_Temple" title="Pashupatinath Temple"><i>Pashupatināth</i></a> upon investigation, was also named <i>Ne</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-Malla_19-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Malla-19">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Norwegian <a href="/wiki/Indology" title="Indology">indologist</a> <a href="/wiki/Christian_Lassen" title="Christian Lassen">Christian Lassen</a> had proposed that <i>Nepāla</i> was a compound of <i>Nipa</i> (foot of a mountain) and <i>-ala</i> (short suffix for <i>alaya</i> meaning abode), and so <i>Nepāla</i> meant "abode at the foot of the mountain". He considered <i>Ne Muni</i> to be a fabrication.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> Indologist <a href="/wiki/Sylvain_Levi" class="mw-redirect" title="Sylvain Levi">Sylvain Levi</a> found Lassen's theory untenable but had no theories of his own, only suggesting that either <i><a href="/wiki/Newar_people" title="Newar people">Newara</a></i> is a <a href="/wiki/Vulgarism" title="Vulgarism">vulgarism</a> of <a href="/wiki/Sanskrit" title="Sanskrit">sanskritic</a> <i>Nepala,</i> or <i>Nepala</i> is <a href="/wiki/Sanskritisation" title="Sanskritisation">Sanskritisation</a> of the local ethnic;<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> his view has found some support though it does not answer the question of etymology.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Malla_19-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Malla-19">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> It has also been proposed that <i>Nepa</i> is a <a href="/wiki/Tibeto-Burman_languages" title="Tibeto-Burman languages">Tibeto-Burman</a> stem consisting of <i>Ne</i> (cattle) and <i>Pa</i> (keeper), reflecting the fact that early inhabitants of the valley were <i>Gopalas</i> (cowherds) and <i><a href="/wiki/Mahisapala_dynasty" title="Mahisapala dynasty">Mahispalas</a></i> (buffalo-herds).<sup id="cite_ref-Malla_19-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Malla-19">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Suniti_Kumar_Chatterji" title="Suniti Kumar Chatterji">Suniti Kumar Chatterji</a> believed <i>Nepal</i> originated from Tibeto-Burman roots – <i>Ne,</i> of uncertain meaning (as multiple possibilities exist), and <i>pala</i> or <i>bal</i>, whose meaning is lost entirely.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="History">History</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/History_of_Nepal" title="History of Nepal">History of Nepal</a></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Ancient_Nepal">Ancient Nepal</span></h3>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1096954695/mw-parser-output/.tmulti">.mw-parser-output .tmulti .multiimageinner{display:flex;flex-direction:column}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .trow{display:flex;flex-direction:row;clear:left;flex-wrap:wrap;width:100%;box-sizing:border-box}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .tsingle{margin:1px;float:left}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .theader{clear:both;font-weight:bold;text-align:center;align-self:center;background-color:transparent;width:100%}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .thumbcaption{background-color:transparent}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .text-align-left{text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .text-align-right{text-align:right}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .text-align-center{text-align:center}@media all and (max-width:720px){.mw-parser-output .tmulti .thumbinner{width:100%!important;box-sizing:border-box;max-width:none!important;align-items:center}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .trow{justify-content:center}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .tsingle{float:none!important;max-width:100%!important;box-sizing:border-box;text-align:center}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .tsingle .thumbcaption{text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .trow>.thumbcaption{text-align:center}}</style><div class="thumb tmulti tleft"><div class="thumbinner multiimageinner" style="width:292px;max-width:292px"><div class="trow"><div class="theader">Ancient Nepal</div></div><div class="trow"><div class="tsingle" style="width:290px;max-width:290px"><div class="thumbimage" style="border:none;;height:183px;overflow:hidden"><a href="/wiki/File:Birth_of_Buddha_at_Lumbini.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9a/Birth_of_Buddha_at_Lumbini.jpg/288px-Birth_of_Buddha_at_Lumbini.jpg" decoding="async" width="288" height="184" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9a/Birth_of_Buddha_at_Lumbini.jpg/432px-Birth_of_Buddha_at_Lumbini.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9a/Birth_of_Buddha_at_Lumbini.jpg/576px-Birth_of_Buddha_at_Lumbini.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1941" data-file-height="1239" /></a></div><div class="thumbcaption">This painting in a <a href="/wiki/Laos" title="Laos">Laotian</a> temple depicts a legend surrounding the birth of <a href="/wiki/Gautama_Buddha" class="mw-redirect" title="Gautama Buddha">Gautama Buddha</a> <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;563</span> BC in <a href="/wiki/Lumbini" title="Lumbini">Lumbini</a>, Western Nepal.</div></div></div><div class="trow"><div class="tsingle" style="width:290px;max-width:290px"><div class="thumbimage" style="border:none;;height:205px;overflow:hidden"><a href="/wiki/File:Changunarayan_photowalk-WLV-3897.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4e/Changunarayan_photowalk-WLV-3897.jpg/288px-Changunarayan_photowalk-WLV-3897.jpg" decoding="async" width="288" height="206" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4e/Changunarayan_photowalk-WLV-3897.jpg/432px-Changunarayan_photowalk-WLV-3897.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4e/Changunarayan_photowalk-WLV-3897.jpg/576px-Changunarayan_photowalk-WLV-3897.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4838" data-file-height="3456" /></a></div><div class="thumbcaption">In the premises of the <a href="/wiki/Changu_Narayan_Temple" title="Changu Narayan Temple">Changu Narayan Temple</a>, is a stone inscription dated 464 AD, the first in Nepal since the Ashoka inscription of Lumbini (<abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;250</span> BC).</div></div></div></div></div>
<p>By 55,000 years ago, the first modern humans had arrived on the Indian subcontinent from Africa, where they had earlier evolved.<sup id="cite_ref-Dyson2018_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Dyson2018-30">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-PetragliaAllchin_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-PetragliaAllchin-31">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Fisher2018_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Fisher2018-32">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> The earliest known modern human remains in <a href="/wiki/South_Asia" title="South Asia">South Asia</a> date to about 30,000 years ago.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> The oldest discovered archaeological evidence of human settlements in Nepal dates to around the same time.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After 6500 BC, evidence for the domestication of food crops and animals, construction of permanent structures, and storage of agricultural surplus appeared in <a href="/wiki/Mehrgarh" title="Mehrgarh">Mehrgarh</a> and other sites in what is now <a href="/wiki/Balochistan" title="Balochistan">Balochistan</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-coni_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-coni-35">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> These gradually developed into the <a href="/wiki/Indus_Valley_civilisation" class="mw-redirect" title="Indus Valley civilisation">Indus Valley civilisation</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-coni_35-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-coni-35">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> the first urban culture in South Asia.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> Prehistoric sites of <a href="/wiki/Paleolithic" title="Paleolithic">palaeolithic</a>, <a href="/wiki/Mesolithic" title="Mesolithic">mesolithic</a> and <a href="/wiki/Neolithic" title="Neolithic">neolithic</a> origins have been discovered in the <a href="/wiki/Sivalik_Hills" title="Sivalik Hills">Siwalik hills</a> of <a href="/wiki/Dang_District,_Nepal" title="Dang District, Nepal">Dang district</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> The earliest inhabitants of modern Nepal and adjoining areas are believed to be people from the <a href="/wiki/Indus_Valley_civilisation" class="mw-redirect" title="Indus Valley civilisation">Indus Valley civilisation</a>. It is possible that the <a href="/wiki/Dravidian_peoples" title="Dravidian peoples">Dravidian</a> people whose history predates the onset of the <a href="/wiki/Bronze_Age" title="Bronze Age">Bronze Age</a> in the Indian subcontinent (around 6300 BC) inhabited the area before the arrival of other ethnic groups like the Tibeto-Burmans and <a href="/wiki/Indo-Aryan_peoples" title="Indo-Aryan peoples">Indo-Aryans</a> from across the border.<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> By 4000 BC, the Tibeto-Burmese people had reached Nepal either directly across the Himalayas from Tibet or via Myanmar and north-east India or both.<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Stella_Kramrisch" title="Stella Kramrisch">Stella Kramrisch</a> (1964) mentions a substratum of a race of pre-Dravidians and Dravidians, who were in Nepal even before the Newars, who formed the majority of the ancient inhabitants of the valley of Kathmandu.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>By the late <a href="/wiki/Vedic_period" title="Vedic period">Vedic period</a>, Nepal was being mentioned in various Hindu texts, such as the late Vedic <i><a href="/wiki/Pari%C5%9Bi%E1%B9%A3%E1%B9%ADa#Atharvaveda" title="Pariśiṣṭa">Atharvaveda Pariśiṣṭa</a></i> and in the post-Vedic <i>Atharvashirsha</i> <a href="/wiki/Upanishads" title="Upanishads">Upanishad</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated4_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated4-42">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Gopal_Bansa" class="mw-redirect" title="Gopal Bansa">Gopal Bansa</a> was the oldest dynasty to be mentioned in various texts as the earliest rulers of the central Himalayan kingdom known by the name 'Nepal'.<sup id="cite_ref-Tiwari2001_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Tiwari2001-43">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> The Gopalas were followed by <a href="/wiki/Kirata_Kingdom" title="Kirata Kingdom">Kiratas</a> who ruled for over 16 centuries by some accounts.<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> According to the <a href="/wiki/Mahabharata" title="Mahabharata">Mahabharata</a>, the then Kirata king went to take part in the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Kurukshetra" class="mw-redirect" title="Battle of Kurukshetra">Battle of Kurukshetra</a>. In the south-eastern region, <a href="/wiki/Janakpurdham" class="mw-redirect" title="Janakpurdham">Janakpurdham</a> was the capital of the prosperous kingdom of <a href="/wiki/Videha" title="Videha">Videha</a> or Mithila, that extended down to the Ganges, and home to <a href="/wiki/King_Janak" class="mw-redirect" title="King Janak">King Janaka</a> and his daughter, <a href="/wiki/Sita" title="Sita">Sita</a>.
</p><p>Around 600 BC, small kingdoms and confederations of clans arose in the southern regions of Nepal. From one of these, the <a href="/wiki/Shakya" title="Shakya">Shakya</a> polity, arose a prince who later renounced his status to lead an ascetic life, founded <a href="/wiki/Buddhism" title="Buddhism">Buddhism</a>, and came to be known as <a href="/wiki/Gautama_Buddha" class="mw-redirect" title="Gautama Buddha">Gautama Buddha</a> (traditionally dated 563–483 BC).<sup id="cite_ref-Klostermaier_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Klostermaier-45">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> Nepal came to be established as a land of spirituality and refuge in the intervening centuries, played an important role in transmitting Buddhism to East Asia via Tibet,<sup id="cite_ref-leo_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-leo-46">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> and helped preserve Hindu and Buddhist manuscripts.
</p><p>By 250 BC, the southern regions had come under the influence of the <a href="/wiki/Maurya_Empire" title="Maurya Empire">Maurya Empire</a>. <a href="/wiki/Emperor_Ashoka" class="mw-redirect" title="Emperor Ashoka">Emperor Ashoka</a> made a pilgrimage to <a href="/wiki/Lumbini" title="Lumbini">Lumbini</a> and <a href="/wiki/Pillars_of_Ashoka" title="Pillars of Ashoka">erected a pillar</a> at Buddha's birthplace, the <a href="/wiki/Lumbini_pillar_inscription" title="Lumbini pillar inscription">inscriptions</a> on which mark the starting point for properly recorded history of Nepal.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELandon192811_47-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELandon192811-47">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> Ashoka also visited the Kathmandu valley and built monuments commemorating Gautama Buddha's visit there. By the 4th century AD, much of Nepal was under the influence of the <a href="/wiki/Gupta_Empire" title="Gupta Empire">Gupta Empire</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;d&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-ChakrabartiChakrabarti2013_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ChakrabartiChakrabarti2013-49">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the Kathmandu valley, the Kiratas were pushed eastward by the <a href="/wiki/Licchavi_(tribe)" title="Licchavi (tribe)">Licchavis</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/Licchavi_(kingdom)" title="Licchavi (kingdom)">Licchavi dynasty</a> came into power <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr> 400 AD. The Lichchhavis built monuments and left a series of inscriptions; Nepal's history of the period is pieced together almost entirely from them.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELandon192819_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELandon192819-50">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-leo_46-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-leo-46">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> In 641, <a href="/wiki/Songtsen_Gampo" title="Songtsen Gampo">Songtsen Gampo</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Tibetan_Empire" title="Tibetan Empire">Tibetan Empire</a> sends Narendradeva back to <a href="/wiki/Licchavi_(kingdom)" title="Licchavi (kingdom)">Licchavi</a> with an army and subjugates Nepal. Parts of Nepal and Licchavi was later under the direct influences of the Tibetan empire.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> The Licchavi dynasty went into decline in the late 8th century and was followed by a <a href="/wiki/Thakuri" title="Thakuri">Thakuri</a> rule. Thakuri kings ruled over the country up to the middle of the 11th century AD; not much is known of this period that is often called the dark period.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Medieval_Nepal">Medieval Nepal</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1096954695/mw-parser-output/.tmulti"/><div class="thumb tmulti tleft"><div class="thumbinner multiimageinner" style="width:292px;max-width:292px"><div class="trow"><div class="theader">Medieval Nepal</div></div><div class="trow"><div class="tsingle" style="width:290px;max-width:290px"><div class="thumbimage" style="border:none;;height:216px;overflow:hidden"><a href="/wiki/File:Sinja_Valley,_Karnali.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/33/Sinja_Valley%2C_Karnali.jpg/288px-Sinja_Valley%2C_Karnali.jpg" decoding="async" width="288" height="216" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/33/Sinja_Valley%2C_Karnali.jpg/432px-Sinja_Valley%2C_Karnali.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/33/Sinja_Valley%2C_Karnali.jpg/576px-Sinja_Valley%2C_Karnali.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3264" data-file-height="2448" /></a></div><div class="thumbcaption"><a href="/wiki/Sinja_Valley" title="Sinja Valley">Sinja Valley</a>, thought to be the place of origin of the <a href="/wiki/Khas" class="mw-redirect" title="Khas">Khasas</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Nepali_language" title="Nepali language">Nepali language</a>, was at the heart of the Khas Malla empire.</div></div></div><div class="trow"><div class="tsingle" style="width:290px;max-width:290px"><div class="thumbimage" style="border:none;;height:162px;overflow:hidden"><a href="/wiki/File:Patan_Durbar_Square-2644.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/17/Patan_Durbar_Square-2644.jpg/288px-Patan_Durbar_Square-2644.jpg" decoding="async" width="288" height="162" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/17/Patan_Durbar_Square-2644.jpg/432px-Patan_Durbar_Square-2644.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/17/Patan_Durbar_Square-2644.jpg/576px-Patan_Durbar_Square-2644.jpg 2x" data-file-width="6000" data-file-height="3375" /></a></div><div class="thumbcaption"><a href="/wiki/Patan_Durbar_Square" title="Patan Durbar Square">Patan Durbar Square</a>, one of the three palace squares in the Kathmandu Valley, was built by the Mallas in the 17th century. The Durbar Squares are a culmination of over a millennium of development in Nepali art and architecture.</div></div></div></div></div>
<p>In the 11th century, a <a href="/wiki/Khasa_Kingdom" title="Khasa Kingdom">powerful empire</a> of <a href="/wiki/Khas_people" title="Khas people">Khas people</a> emerged in western Nepal whose territory at its highest peak included much of western Nepal as well as parts of western Tibet and <a href="/wiki/Uttarakhand" title="Uttarakhand">Uttarakhand</a> of India. By the 14th century, the empire had splintered into loosely associated <span title="Nepali (macrolanguage)-language romanization"><i lang="ne-Latn"><a href="/wiki/Baise_rajya" class="mw-redirect" title="Baise rajya">Baise rajyas</a></i></span>, literally 22 states as they were counted. The rich culture and language of the Khas people spread throughout Nepal and as far as Indo-China in the intervening centuries; <a href="/wiki/Khas_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Khas language">their language</a>, later renamed the Nepali language, became the lingua franca of Nepal as well as much of North-east India.<sup id="cite_ref-leo_46-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-leo-46">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In south-eastern Nepal, Simraungarh annexed <a href="/wiki/Mithila_(region)" title="Mithila (region)">Mithila</a> around 1100 AD, and the unified Tirhut stood as a powerful kingdom for more than 200 years,<sup id="cite_ref-Darnal_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Darnal-53">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> even ruling over Kathmandu for a time.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELandon192832–33_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELandon192832–33-54">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> After another 300 years of Muslim rule, Tirhut came under the control of the Sens of <a href="/wiki/Makwanpur_District" title="Makwanpur District">Makawanpur</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Darnal_53-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Darnal-53">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> In the eastern hills, a confederation of Kirat principalities ruled the area between Kathmandu and Bengal.
</p><p>In the Kathmandu valley, the <a href="/wiki/Malla_(Nepal)" class="mw-redirect" title="Malla (Nepal)">Mallas</a>, who make several appearances in Nepalese history since ancient times, had established themselves in Kathmandu and Patan by the middle of the 14th century. The Mallas ruled the valley first under the suzerainty of Tirhut but established independent reign by late 14th century as Tirhut went into decline. In the late 14th century, <a href="/wiki/Jayasthiti_Malla" title="Jayasthiti Malla">Jayasthiti Malla</a> introduced widespread socio-economic reforms, principal of which was the caste system. By dividing the indigenous non-Aryan Buddhist population into castes modelled after the four <a href="/wiki/Varna_(Hinduism)" title="Varna (Hinduism)">Varna system</a> of Hinduism, he provided an influential model for the Sanskritisation and Hinduisation of the indigenous non-Hindu tribal populations in all principalities throughout Nepal. By the middle of the 15th century, Kathmandu had become a powerful empire which, according to <a href="/wiki/William_James_Kirkpatrick" class="mw-redirect" title="William James Kirkpatrick">Kirkpatrick</a>, extended from Digarchi or Sigatse in Tibet to Tirhut and Gaya in India.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELandon192839_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELandon192839-55">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> In the late 15th century, Malla princes divided their kingdom in four – Kathmandu, Patan and Bhaktapur in the valley and Banepa to the east. The competition for prestige among these brotherly kingdoms saw the flourishing of art and architecture in central Nepal, and the building of famous <a href="/wiki/Kathmandu_Durbar_Square" title="Kathmandu Durbar Square">Kathmandu</a>, <a href="/wiki/Patan_Durbar_Square" title="Patan Durbar Square">Patan</a> and <a href="/wiki/Bhaktapur_Durbar_Square" title="Bhaktapur Durbar Square">Bhaktapur Durbar Squares</a>; their division and mistrust led to their fall in the late 18th century, and ultimately, the unification of Nepal into a modern state.<sup id="cite_ref-leo_46-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-leo-46">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELandon192852–61_56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELandon192852–61-56">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Apart from one destructive sacking of Kathmandu in the early 13th century, Nepal remains largely untouched by the <a href="/wiki/Muslim_invasion_of_India" class="mw-redirect" title="Muslim invasion of India">Muslim invasion of India</a> that began in the 11th century. The <a href="/wiki/Mughal_Empire" title="Mughal Empire">Mughal period</a> saw an influx of high-caste Hindus from India into Nepal. They soon intermingled with the Khas people and by the 16th century, there were about 50 Rajput-ruled principalities in Nepal, including the 22 (Baisi) states and, to their east in west-central Nepal, 24 <a href="/wiki/Chaubisi_rajya" class="mw-redirect" title="Chaubisi rajya"><i>Chaubisi</i> states</a>. There emerged a view that Nepal remained the true bastion of unadulterated Hinduism at a time when Indian culture had been influenced by centuries of Mughal, followed by <a href="/wiki/British_Raj" title="British Raj">British rule</a>. <a href="/wiki/Gorkha_Kingdom" title="Gorkha Kingdom">Gorkha</a>, one of the Baisi states, emerged as an influential and ambitious kingdom with a reputation for justice, after it codified the first Hinduism-based laws in the Nepalese hills.<sup id="cite_ref-leo_46-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-leo-46">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Unification.2C_expansion_and_consolidation_.281768.E2.80.931951.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Unification,_expansion_and_consolidation_(1768–1951)">Unification, expansion and consolidation (1768–1951)</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Nepal" title="Kingdom of Nepal">Kingdom of Nepal</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1096954695/mw-parser-output/.tmulti"/><div class="thumb tmulti tright" style="background-color:none"><div class="thumbinner multiimageinner" style="width:352px;max-width:352px;background-color:none"><div class="trow"><div class="tsingle" style="background-color:none;width:188px;max-width:188px"><div class="thumbimage" style="border:none;;height:185px;overflow:hidden"><a href="/wiki/File:Prithvi_Narayan_Shah.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/99/Prithvi_Narayan_Shah.jpg/186px-Prithvi_Narayan_Shah.jpg" decoding="async" width="186" height="186" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/99/Prithvi_Narayan_Shah.jpg/279px-Prithvi_Narayan_Shah.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/99/Prithvi_Narayan_Shah.jpg/372px-Prithvi_Narayan_Shah.jpg 2x" data-file-width="796" data-file-height="796" /></a></div><div class="thumbcaption text-align-center"><a href="/wiki/Prithvi_Narayan_Shah" title="Prithvi Narayan Shah">Prithvi Narayan Shah</a>, began the <a href="/wiki/Unification_of_Nepal" title="Unification of Nepal">unification process</a> of what would become the present-day country of Nepal</div></div><div class="tsingle" style="background-color:none;width:160px;max-width:160px"><div class="thumbimage" style="border:none;;height:185px;overflow:hidden"><a href="/wiki/File:Bhimsen-thapa-painting_(cropped).jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e6/Bhimsen-thapa-painting_%28cropped%29.jpg/158px-Bhimsen-thapa-painting_%28cropped%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="158" height="186" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/e/e6/Bhimsen-thapa-painting_%28cropped%29.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="165" data-file-height="194" /></a></div><div class="thumbcaption text-align-center">During <a href="/wiki/Bhimsen_Thapa" title="Bhimsen Thapa">Bhimsen Thapa</a>'s premiership Nepal reached its zenith</div></div></div><div class="trow"><div class="tsingle" style="background-color:none;width:173px;max-width:173px"><div class="thumbimage" style="border:none;;height:242px;overflow:hidden"><a href="/wiki/File:Jung_Bahadur_Rana_in_1887.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/13/Jung_Bahadur_Rana_in_1887.jpg/171px-Jung_Bahadur_Rana_in_1887.jpg" decoding="async" width="171" height="243" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/13/Jung_Bahadur_Rana_in_1887.jpg/257px-Jung_Bahadur_Rana_in_1887.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/13/Jung_Bahadur_Rana_in_1887.jpg/342px-Jung_Bahadur_Rana_in_1887.jpg 2x" data-file-width="5636" data-file-height="8000" /></a></div><div class="thumbcaption text-align-center"><a href="/wiki/Jung_Bahadur_Rana" title="Jung Bahadur Rana">Jung Bahadur Rana</a>, who established the autocratic <a href="/wiki/Rana_regime" class="mw-redirect" title="Rana regime">Rana regime</a> in 1846 and instituted a pro-British foreign policy</div></div><div class="tsingle" style="background-color:none;width:175px;max-width:175px"><div class="thumbimage" style="border:none;;height:242px;overflow:hidden"><a href="/wiki/File:King_Mahendra_(cropped).png" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dc/King_Mahendra_%28cropped%29.png/173px-King_Mahendra_%28cropped%29.png" decoding="async" width="173" height="241" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dc/King_Mahendra_%28cropped%29.png/260px-King_Mahendra_%28cropped%29.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dc/King_Mahendra_%28cropped%29.png/346px-King_Mahendra_%28cropped%29.png 2x" data-file-width="2115" data-file-height="2952" /></a></div><div class="thumbcaption text-align-center">During King <a href="/wiki/Mahendra_of_Nepal" title="Mahendra of Nepal">Mahendra</a>'s reign, Nepal experienced a period of industrial, political, and economic change.</div></div></div></div></div>
<p>In the mid-18th century, <a href="/wiki/Prithvi_Narayan_Shah" title="Prithvi Narayan Shah">Prithvi Narayan Shah</a>, a <a href="/wiki/Gurkha" title="Gurkha">Gorkha</a> king, set out to put together what would become present-day Nepal. He embarked on his mission by securing the neutrality of the bordering mountain kingdoms. After several bloody battles and sieges, notably the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Kirtipur" title="Battle of Kirtipur">Battle of Kirtipur</a>, he managed to conquer the Kathmandu Valley in 1769.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
The Gorkha control reached its height when the <a href="/wiki/Kumaon_Kingdom" title="Kumaon Kingdom">Kumaon</a> and <a href="/wiki/Garhwal_Kingdom" title="Garhwal Kingdom">Garhwal Kingdoms</a> in the west to <a href="/wiki/Sikkim" title="Sikkim">Sikkim</a> in the east came under Nepalese control. A dispute with <a href="/wiki/Tibet" title="Tibet">Tibet</a> over the control of mountain passes and inner Tingri valleys of Tibet prompted the <a href="/wiki/Qing_dynasty" title="Qing dynasty">Qing Emperor</a> of China to start the <a href="/wiki/Sino-Nepalese_War" title="Sino-Nepalese War">Sino-Nepali War</a> compelling the Nepali to retreat to their own borders in the north.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELandon192868–69_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELandon192868–69-58">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> The rivalry between the Kingdom of Nepal and the <a href="/wiki/East_India_Company" title="East India Company">East India Company</a> over the control of states bordering Nepal eventually led to the <a href="/wiki/Anglo-Nepalese_War" title="Anglo-Nepalese War">Anglo-Nepali War</a> (1815–16). At first, the British underestimated the Nepali and were soundly defeated until committing more military resources than they had anticipated needing. Thus began the reputation of <a href="/wiki/Gurkha" title="Gurkha">Gurkhas</a> as fierce and ruthless soldiers. The war ended in the <a href="/wiki/Sugauli_Treaty" class="mw-redirect" title="Sugauli Treaty">Sugauli Treaty</a>, under which Nepal ceded recently captured lands.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELandon192875–80_59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELandon192875–80-59">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-leo_46-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-leo-46">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Factionalism inside the royal family led to a period of instability. In 1846, a plot was discovered revealing that the reigning queen had planned to overthrow Jung Bahadur Kunwar, a fast-rising military leader. This led to the <a href="/wiki/Kot_massacre" title="Kot massacre">Kot massacre</a>; armed clashes between military personnel and administrators loyal to the queen led to the execution of several hundred princes and chieftains around the country. Bir Narsingh Kunwar emerged victoriously and founded the <a href="/wiki/Rana_dynasty" title="Rana dynasty">Rana dynasty</a>, and came to be known as <a href="/wiki/Jung_Bahadur_Rana" title="Jung Bahadur Rana">Jung Bahadur Rana</a>. The king was made a titular figure, and the post of Prime Minister was made powerful and hereditary. The Ranas were staunchly pro-British and assisted them during the <a href="/wiki/Indian_Rebellion_of_1857" title="Indian Rebellion of 1857">Indian Rebellion of 1857</a> (and later in both World Wars). In 1860 some parts of the western Terai region were gifted to Nepal by the British as a friendly gesture because of her military help to sustain British control in India during the rebellion (known as <i><a href="/wiki/Naya_Muluk" title="Naya Muluk">Naya Muluk</a></i>, new country). In 1923, the United Kingdom and Nepal formally signed <a href="/wiki/Nepal%E2%80%93Britain_Treaty_of_1923" title="Nepal–Britain Treaty of 1923">an agreement of friendship</a> that superseded the Sugauli Treaty of 1816.<sup id="cite_ref-auto_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto-60">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Hindu practice of <a href="/wiki/Sati_(practice)" title="Sati (practice)">Sati</a>, in which a widow sacrificed herself in the funeral pyre of her husband, was banned in 1919, and <a href="/wiki/History_of_slavery_in_Asia" class="mw-redirect" title="History of slavery in Asia">slavery</a> was officially abolished in 1924.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHutt200422_61-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHutt200422-61">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> Rana rule was marked by tyranny, <a href="https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/debauchery" class="extiw" title="wikt:debauchery">debauchery</a>, economic exploitation and religious persecution.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Contemporary_history">Contemporary history</span></h3>
<p>In the late 1940s, newly emerging pro-democracy movements and political parties in Nepal were critical of the Rana autocracy. Following the success of Indian Independence Movement which Nepalese activists had taken part in, with India's support and cooperation of King Tribhuvan, <a href="/wiki/Nepali_Congress" title="Nepali Congress">Nepali Congress</a> was successful in toppling the Rana regime, establishing a parliamentary democracy. After a decade of power wrangling between the king and the government, <a href="/wiki/Mahendra_of_Nepal" title="Mahendra of Nepal">King Mahendra</a> (ruled 1955–1972) scrapped the democratic experiment in 1960, and a "partyless" <a href="/wiki/Panchayat_(Nepal)" title="Panchayat (Nepal)">Panchayat</a> system was made to govern Nepal. The political parties were banned and politicians imprisoned or exiled.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHutt20043–7_64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHutt20043–7-64">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> The Panchayat rule modernised the country, introducing reforms and developing infrastructure, but curtailed liberties and imposed heavy censorship. In 1990, the <a href="/wiki/People%27s_Movement_I_(1990)" class="mw-redirect" title="People&#39;s Movement I (1990)">People's Movement</a> forced <a href="/wiki/Birendra_of_Nepal" title="Birendra of Nepal">King Birendra</a> (ruled 1972–2001) to accept constitutional reforms and to establish a multiparty democracy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHutt20043–7_64-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHutt20043–7-64">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Nepal_Timeline_65-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Nepal_Timeline-65">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1996, the <a href="/wiki/Unified_Communist_Party_of_Nepal_(Maoist)" class="mw-redirect" title="Unified Communist Party of Nepal (Maoist)">Maoist Party</a> started a violent bid to replace the royal parliamentary system with a people's republic.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHutt20043–7_64-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHutt20043–7-64">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> This led to the long <a href="/wiki/Nepali_Civil_War" class="mw-redirect" title="Nepali Civil War">Nepali Civil War</a> and more than 16,000 deaths.<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> With the death of both the King and the Crown Prince in a <a href="/wiki/Nepalese_royal_massacre" title="Nepalese royal massacre">massacre in the royal palace</a>, King Birendra's brother <a href="/wiki/Gyanendra_of_Nepal" title="Gyanendra of Nepal">Gyanendra</a> inherited the throne in 2001<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHutt20043–7_64-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHutt20043–7-64">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> and subsequently assumed full executive powers aiming to quash the Maoist insurgency himself.<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Maoist Party joined mainstream politics following the success of the peaceful <a href="/wiki/2006_Nepalese_revolution" title="2006 Nepalese revolution">democratic revolution of 2006</a>;<sup id="cite_ref-Abolish_68-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Abolish-68">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> Nepal became a <a href="/wiki/Secular_state" title="Secular state">secular state</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> and on 28 May 2008, it was declared a federal republic,<sup id="cite_ref-Abolish_68-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Abolish-68">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> ending its time-honoured status as the world's only Hindu kingdom.<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> After a decade of instability and internal strife which saw two constituent assembly elections, the <a href="/wiki/Constitution_of_Nepal" title="Constitution of Nepal">new constitution</a> was promulgated on 20 September 2015, making Nepal a federal democratic republic divided into <a href="/wiki/Provinces_of_Nepal" title="Provinces of Nepal">seven provinces</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div style="clear:both;"></div>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Geography">Geography</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Geography_of_Nepal" title="Geography of Nepal">Geography of Nepal</a> and <a href="/wiki/Geology_of_Nepal" title="Geology of Nepal">Geology of Nepal</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Nepal_topo_en.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/23/Nepal_topo_en.jpg/220px-Nepal_topo_en.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="134" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/23/Nepal_topo_en.jpg/330px-Nepal_topo_en.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/23/Nepal_topo_en.jpg/440px-Nepal_topo_en.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1400" data-file-height="852" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Nepal_topo_en.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>A topographic map of Nepal</div></div></div>
<p>Nepal is of roughly trapezoidal shape, about 800 kilometres (500&#160;mi) long and 200 kilometres (120&#160;mi) wide, with an area of 147,516&#160;km<sup>2</sup> (56,956&#160;sq&#160;mi). It lies between latitudes <a href="/wiki/26th_parallel_north" title="26th parallel north">26°</a> and <a href="/wiki/31st_parallel_north" title="31st parallel north">31°N</a>, and longitudes <a href="/wiki/80th_meridian_east" title="80th meridian east">80°</a> and <a href="/wiki/89th_meridian_east" title="89th meridian east">89°E</a>. Nepal's defining geological processes began 75&#160;million years ago when the Indian plate, then part of the southern supercontinent <a href="/wiki/Gondwana" title="Gondwana">Gondwana</a>, began a north-eastward <a href="/wiki/Plate_tectonics" title="Plate tectonics">drift</a> caused by <a href="/wiki/Seafloor_spreading" title="Seafloor spreading">seafloor spreading</a> to its south-west, and later, south and south-east.<sup id="cite_ref-ali_73-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ali-73">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> Simultaneously, the vast <a href="/wiki/Tethys_Ocean" title="Tethys Ocean">Tethyn</a> <a href="/wiki/Oceanic_crust" title="Oceanic crust">oceanic crust</a>, to its northeast, began to <a href="/wiki/Subduction" title="Subduction">subduct</a> under the <a href="/wiki/Eurasian_plate" class="mw-redirect" title="Eurasian plate">Eurasian plate</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ali_73-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ali-73">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> These dual processes, driven by convection in the Earth's <a href="/wiki/Mantle_(geology)" title="Mantle (geology)">mantle</a>, both created the <a href="/wiki/Indian_Ocean" title="Indian Ocean">Indian Ocean</a> and caused the Indian <a href="/wiki/Continental_crust" title="Continental crust">continental crust</a> eventually to under-thrust Eurasia and to uplift the <a href="/wiki/Himalayas" title="Himalayas">Himalayas</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ali_73-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ali-73">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> The rising barriers blocked the paths of rivers creating large lakes, which only broke through as late as 100,000 years ago, creating fertile valleys in the middle hills like the Kathmandu Valley. In the western region, rivers which were too strong to be hampered, cut some of the world's deepest gorges.<sup id="cite_ref-Jwhelpton_74-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Jwhelpton-74">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> Immediately south of the emerging Himalayas, plate movement created a vast <a href="/wiki/Trough_(geology)" title="Trough (geology)">trough</a> that rapidly filled with river-borne sediment<sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> and now constitutes the <a href="/wiki/Indo-Gangetic_Plain" title="Indo-Gangetic Plain">Indo-Gangetic Plain</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> Nepal lies almost completely within this collision zone, occupying the central sector of the Himalayan arc, nearly one-third of the 2,400&#160;km (1,500&#160;mi)-long Himalayas,<sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> with a small strip of southernmost Nepal stretching into the Indo-Gangetic plain and two districts in the northwest stretching up to the Tibetan plateau.<sup id="cite_ref-Jwhelpton_74-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Jwhelpton-74">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Everest_kalapatthar_crop.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4b/Everest_kalapatthar_crop.jpg/220px-Everest_kalapatthar_crop.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="269" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4b/Everest_kalapatthar_crop.jpg/330px-Everest_kalapatthar_crop.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4b/Everest_kalapatthar_crop.jpg/440px-Everest_kalapatthar_crop.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2299" data-file-height="2811" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Everest_kalapatthar_crop.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Mount_Everest" title="Mount Everest">Mount Everest</a>, the highest peak on earth, lies on the Nepal–China border.</div></div></div>
<p>Nepal is divided into three principal physiographic belts known as <i><a href="/wiki/Himalayas" title="Himalayas">Himal</a></i>–<i><a href="/wiki/Lower_Himalayan_Range" title="Lower Himalayan Range">Pahad</a></i>–<i><a href="/wiki/Terai" title="Terai">Terai</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;e&#93;</a></sup> Himal is the mountain region containing snow and situated in the Great Himalayan Range; it makes up the northern part of Nepal. It contains the highest elevations in the world including 8,848.86 metres (29,032&#160;ft) height <a href="/wiki/Mount_Everest" title="Mount Everest">Mount Everest</a> (<i>Sagarmāthā</i> in Nepali) on the border with China. Seven other of the world's "<a href="/wiki/Eight-thousander" title="Eight-thousander">eight-thousanders</a>" are in Nepal or on its border with Tibet: <a href="/wiki/Lhotse" title="Lhotse">Lhotse</a>, <a href="/wiki/Makalu" title="Makalu">Makalu</a>, <a href="/wiki/Cho_Oyu" title="Cho Oyu">Cho Oyu</a>, <a href="/wiki/Kangchenjunga" title="Kangchenjunga">Kangchenjunga</a>, <a href="/wiki/Dhaulagiri" title="Dhaulagiri">Dhaulagiri</a>, <a href="/wiki/Annapurna" title="Annapurna">Annapurna</a> and <a href="/wiki/Manaslu" title="Manaslu">Manaslu</a>. Pahad is the mountain region that does not generally contain snow. The mountains vary from 800 to 4,000 metres (2,600 to 13,100&#160;ft) in altitude, with progression from subtropical climates below 1,200 metres (3,900&#160;ft) to alpine climates above 3,600 metres (11,800&#160;ft). The <a href="/wiki/Lower_Himalayan_Range" title="Lower Himalayan Range">Lower Himalayan Range</a>, reaching 1,500 to 3,000 metres (4,900 to 9,800&#160;ft), is the southern limit of this region, with subtropical river valleys and "hills" alternating to the north of this range. Population density is high in valleys but notably less above 2,000 metres (6,600&#160;ft) and very low above 2,500 metres (8,200&#160;ft), where snow occasionally falls in winter. The southern lowland plains or <i>Terai</i> bordering India are part of the northern rim of the <a href="/wiki/Indo-Gangetic_Plain" title="Indo-Gangetic Plain">Indo-Gangetic Plain</a>. Terai is the lowland region containing some hill ranges. The plains were formed and are fed by three major Himalayan rivers: the <a href="/wiki/Koshi_River" class="mw-redirect" title="Koshi River">Koshi</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Gandaki_River" title="Gandaki River">Narayani</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/Ghaghara" title="Ghaghara">Karnali</a> as well as smaller rivers rising below the permanent snowline. This region has a subtropical to tropical climate. The outermost range of the foothills called <a href="/wiki/Sivalik_Hills" title="Sivalik Hills">Sivalik Hills</a> or Churia Range, cresting at 700 to 1,000 metres (2,300 to 3,280&#160;ft), marks the limits of the Gangetic Plain. Broad, low valleys called <a href="/wiki/Inner_Terai_Valleys_of_Nepal" title="Inner Terai Valleys of Nepal">Inner Terai Valleys</a> (<span title="Nepali (macrolanguage)-language text"><i lang="ne">Bhitri Tarai Upatyaka</i></span>) lie north of these foothills in several places.
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:K%C3%B6ppen_climate_types_of_Nepal.svg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/48/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_types_of_Nepal.svg/220px-K%C3%B6ppen_climate_types_of_Nepal.svg.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="242" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/48/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_types_of_Nepal.svg/330px-K%C3%B6ppen_climate_types_of_Nepal.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/48/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_types_of_Nepal.svg/440px-K%C3%B6ppen_climate_types_of_Nepal.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="960" data-file-height="1056" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:K%C3%B6ppen_climate_types_of_Nepal.svg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Köppen climate classification for Nepal</div></div></div>
<p>The Indian plate continues to move north relative to Asia at about 50&#160;mm (2.0&#160;in) per year.<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> This makes Nepal an earthquake-prone zone, and periodic earthquakes that have devastating consequences present a significant hurdle to development.<sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> Erosion of the Himalayas is a very important source of sediment, which flows to the Indian Ocean.<sup id="cite_ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> Saptakoshi, in particular, carries a huge amount of silt out of Nepal but sees extreme drop in Gradient in <a href="/wiki/Bihar" title="Bihar">Bihar</a>, causing severe floods and course changes, and is, therefore, known as the sorrow of Bihar. Severe flooding and landslides cause deaths and disease, destroy farmlands and cripple the transport infrastructure of the country, during the monsoon season each year.
</p><p>Nepal has five climatic zones, broadly corresponding to the altitudes. The tropical and subtropical zones lie below 1,200 metres (3,900&#160;ft), the <a href="/wiki/Temperate" class="mw-redirect" title="Temperate">temperate</a> zone 1,200 to 2,400 metres (3,900 to 7,900&#160;ft), the cold zone 2,400 to 3,600 metres (7,900 to 11,800&#160;ft), the subarctic zone 3,600 to 4,400 metres (11,800 to 14,400&#160;ft), and the Arctic zone above 4,400 metres (14,400&#160;ft). Nepal experiences five seasons: summer, <a href="/wiki/Monsoon" title="Monsoon">monsoon</a>, autumn, winter and spring. The Himalayas block cold winds from Central Asia in the winter and form the northern limits of the monsoon wind patterns.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Biodiversity">Biodiversity</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Wildlife_of_Nepal" title="Wildlife of Nepal">Wildlife of Nepal</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/List_of_protected_areas_of_Nepal" title="List of protected areas of Nepal">List of protected areas of Nepal</a> and <a href="/wiki/Community_forestry_in_Nepal" title="Community forestry in Nepal">Community forestry in Nepal</a></div>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Land_cover_map_of_Nepal_using_Landsat_30_m_(2010)_data.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fb/Land_cover_map_of_Nepal_using_Landsat_30_m_%282010%29_data.jpg/220px-Land_cover_map_of_Nepal_using_Landsat_30_m_%282010%29_data.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="110" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fb/Land_cover_map_of_Nepal_using_Landsat_30_m_%282010%29_data.jpg/330px-Land_cover_map_of_Nepal_using_Landsat_30_m_%282010%29_data.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fb/Land_cover_map_of_Nepal_using_Landsat_30_m_%282010%29_data.jpg/440px-Land_cover_map_of_Nepal_using_Landsat_30_m_%282010%29_data.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2400" data-file-height="1200" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Land_cover_map_of_Nepal_using_Landsat_30_m_(2010)_data.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>This land cover map of Nepal using Landsat 30&#160;m (2010) data shows forest cover as the dominant type of land cover in Nepal.<sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup></div></div></div>
<p>Nepal contains a disproportionately large diversity of plants and animals, relative to its size.<sup id="cite_ref-wwf_88-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-wwf-88">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-kindl_89-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-kindl-89">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup> Nepal, in its entirety, forms the western portion of the <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Himalaya" class="mw-redirect" title="Eastern Himalaya">eastern Himalayan</a> biodiversity hotspot, with notable <a href="/wiki/Biocultural_diversity" title="Biocultural diversity">biocultural diversity</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-O&#39;Neill_al2017_90-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-O&#39;Neill_al2017-90">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> The dramatic differences in elevation found in Nepal (60&#160;m from sea level in the Terai plains, to 8,848&#160;m <a href="/wiki/Mount_Everest" title="Mount Everest">Mount Everest</a>)<sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup> result in a variety of <a href="/wiki/Biome" title="Biome">biomes</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-wwf_88-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-wwf-88">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> The Eastern half of Nepal is richer in biodiversity as it receives more rain, compared to western parts, where <a href="/wiki/Arctic_desert" title="Arctic desert">arctic desert</a>-type conditions are more common at higher elevations.<sup id="cite_ref-kindl_89-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-kindl-89">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup> Nepal is a habitat for 4.0% of all <a href="/wiki/Mammal" title="Mammal">mammal species</a>, 8.9% of <a href="/wiki/Bird" title="Bird">bird species</a>, 1.0% of <a href="/wiki/Reptile" title="Reptile">reptile species</a>, 2.5% of <a href="/wiki/Amphibian" title="Amphibian">amphibian species</a>, 1.9% of <a href="/wiki/Fish" title="Fish">fish species</a>, 3.7% of <a href="/wiki/Butterfly" title="Butterfly">butterfly species</a>, 0.5% of <a href="/wiki/Moth" title="Moth">moth species</a> and 0.4% of <a href="/wiki/Spider" title="Spider">spider species</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-kindl_89-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-kindl-89">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup> In its 35 forest-types and 118 ecosystems,<sup id="cite_ref-wwf_88-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-wwf-88">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;f&#93;</a></sup> Nepal harbours 2% of the <a href="/wiki/Flowering_plant" title="Flowering plant">flowering plant species</a>, 3% of <a href="/wiki/Pteridophyte" title="Pteridophyte">pteridophytes</a> and 6% of <a href="/wiki/Bryophyte" title="Bryophyte">bryophytes</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-kindl_89-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-kindl-89">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Indian_rhinoceros_(Rhinoceros_unicornis)_4.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ec/Indian_rhinoceros_%28Rhinoceros_unicornis%29_4.jpg/220px-Indian_rhinoceros_%28Rhinoceros_unicornis%29_4.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ec/Indian_rhinoceros_%28Rhinoceros_unicornis%29_4.jpg/330px-Indian_rhinoceros_%28Rhinoceros_unicornis%29_4.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ec/Indian_rhinoceros_%28Rhinoceros_unicornis%29_4.jpg/440px-Indian_rhinoceros_%28Rhinoceros_unicornis%29_4.jpg 2x" data-file-width="5246" data-file-height="3497" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Indian_rhinoceros_(Rhinoceros_unicornis)_4.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The <a href="/wiki/Greater_one-horned_rhinoceros" class="mw-redirect" title="Greater one-horned rhinoceros">greater one-horned rhinoceros</a> roams the sub-tropical grasslands of the Terai plains.</div></div></div>
<p>Nepal's <a href="/wiki/Forest_cover" title="Forest cover">forest cover</a> is 59,624&#160;km<sup>2</sup> (23,021&#160;sq&#160;mi), 40.36% of the country's total land area, with an additional 4.38% of <a href="/wiki/Shrubland" title="Shrubland">scrubland</a>, for a total forested area of 44.74%, an increase of 5% since the turn of the millennium.<sup id="cite_ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> The country had a 2019 <a href="/wiki/Forest_Landscape_Integrity_Index" title="Forest Landscape Integrity Index">Forest Landscape Integrity Index</a> mean score of 7.23/10, ranking it 45th globally out of 172 countries.<sup id="cite_ref-FLII-Supplementary_94-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FLII-Supplementary-94">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> In the southern plains, <a href="/wiki/Terai%E2%80%93Duar_savanna_and_grasslands" title="Terai–Duar savanna and grasslands">Terai–Duar savanna and grasslands</a> ecoregion contains some of the world's tallest grasses as well as <i><a href="/wiki/Shorea_robusta" title="Shorea robusta">Sal</a></i> forests, <a href="/wiki/Tropical_and_subtropical_moist_broadleaf_forests" title="Tropical and subtropical moist broadleaf forests">tropical evergreen forests</a> and tropical riverine deciduous forests.<sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup> In the lower hills (700&#160;m – 2,000&#160;m), <a href="/wiki/Himalayan_subtropical_broadleaf_forests" title="Himalayan subtropical broadleaf forests">subtropical</a> and <a href="/wiki/Temperate_broadleaf_and_mixed_forest" class="mw-redirect" title="Temperate broadleaf and mixed forest">temperate deciduous mixed forests</a> containing mostly S<i>al</i> (in the lower altitudes), <i><a href="/wiki/Schima_wallichii" title="Schima wallichii">Chilaune</a></i> and <i><a href="/wiki/Castanopsis_indica" title="Castanopsis indica">Katus</a></i>, as well as <a href="/wiki/Tropical_and_subtropical_coniferous_forests" title="Tropical and subtropical coniferous forests">subtropical pine forest</a> dominated by <a href="/wiki/Chir_pine" class="mw-redirect" title="Chir pine">chir pine</a> are common. The middle hills (2,000&#160;m – 3,000&#160;m) are dominated by <a href="/wiki/Oak" title="Oak">oak</a> and <a href="/wiki/Rhododendron" title="Rhododendron">rhododendron</a>. <a href="/wiki/Temperate_coniferous_forest" title="Temperate coniferous forest">Subalpine coniferous forests</a> cover the 3,000&#160;m to 3,500&#160;m range, dominated by oak (particularly in the west), <a href="/wiki/Abies_spectabilis" title="Abies spectabilis">Eastern Himalayan fir</a>, <a href="/wiki/Pinus_wallichiana" title="Pinus wallichiana">Himalayan pine</a> and <a href="/wiki/Tsuga_dumosa" title="Tsuga dumosa">Himalayan hemlock</a>; rhododendron is common as well. Above 3,500&#160;m in the west and 4,000&#160;m in the east, coniferous trees give way to rhododendron-dominated <a href="/wiki/Alpine_tundra" title="Alpine tundra">alpine shrubs and meadows</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-kindl_89-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-kindl-89">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Among the notable trees, are the <a href="/wiki/Astringent" title="Astringent">astringent</a> <i><a href="/wiki/Azadirachta_indica" title="Azadirachta indica">Azadirachta indica</a></i>, or <i>neem</i>, which is widely used in traditional <a href="/wiki/Herbal_medicine" title="Herbal medicine">herbal medicine</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-Goyal2006_96-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Goyal2006-96">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup> and the luxuriant <i><a href="/wiki/Ficus_religiosa" title="Ficus religiosa">Ficus religiosa</a></i>, or <i>peepal</i>,<sup id="cite_ref-Hughes2013_97-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hughes2013-97">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> which is displayed on the ancient seals of <a href="/wiki/Mohenjo-daro" title="Mohenjo-daro">Mohenjo-daro</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-AmeriCostello2018_98-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-AmeriCostello2018-98">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup> and under which <a href="/wiki/Gautama_Buddha" class="mw-redirect" title="Gautama Buddha">Gautam Buddha</a> is recorded in the <a href="/wiki/Pali_canon" class="mw-redirect" title="Pali canon">Pali canon</a> to have sought enlightenment.<sup id="cite_ref-Gwynne2011_99-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Gwynne2011-99">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Lophophorus_impejanus_Zoo_DU_2.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/48/Lophophorus_impejanus_Zoo_DU_2.jpg/220px-Lophophorus_impejanus_Zoo_DU_2.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="174" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/48/Lophophorus_impejanus_Zoo_DU_2.jpg/330px-Lophophorus_impejanus_Zoo_DU_2.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/48/Lophophorus_impejanus_Zoo_DU_2.jpg/440px-Lophophorus_impejanus_Zoo_DU_2.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3171" data-file-height="2509" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Lophophorus_impejanus_Zoo_DU_2.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>The <a href="/wiki/Himalayan_monal" title="Himalayan monal">Himalayan monal</a> (<span title="Nepali (macrolanguage)-language text"><i lang="ne">Danphe</i></span>), the national bird of Nepal,<sup id="cite_ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup> nests high in the Himalayas.</div></div></div>
<p>Most of the subtropical evergreen broad-leaved forest of the lower Himalayan region is descended from the <a href="/wiki/Tethys_Ocean" title="Tethys Ocean">Tethyan</a> <a href="/wiki/Tertiary" title="Tertiary">Tertiary</a> flora.<sup id="cite_ref-sun_101-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-sun-101">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> As the <a href="/wiki/Indian_Plate" title="Indian Plate">Indian Plate</a> collided with <a href="/wiki/Eurasia" title="Eurasia">Eurasia</a> forming and raising the Himalayas, the <a href="/wiki/Arid" title="Arid">arid</a> and semi-arid <a href="/wiki/Mediterranean_flora" class="mw-redirect" title="Mediterranean flora">Mediterranean flora</a> was pushed up and adapted to the more alpine climate over the next 40–50&#160;million years.<sup id="cite_ref-sun_101-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-sun-101">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-USGS_102-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-USGS-102">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Himalaya" class="mw-redirect" title="Eastern Himalaya">Himalayan biodiversity hotspot</a> was the site of mass exchange and intermingling of the Indian and Eurasian species in the <a href="/wiki/Neogene" title="Neogene">neogene</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup> One mammal species (<a href="/wiki/Himalayan_field_mouse" title="Himalayan field mouse">Himalayan field mouse</a>), two each of bird and reptile species, nine amphibia, eight fish and 29 butterfly species are endemic to Nepal.<sup id="cite_ref-kindl_89-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-kindl-89">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-105">&#91;g&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Nepal contains 107 <a href="/wiki/IUCN" class="mw-redirect" title="IUCN">IUCN</a>-designated <a href="/wiki/Threatened_species" title="Threatened species">threatened species</a>, 88 of them animal species, 18 plant species and one species of "fungi or protist" group.<sup id="cite_ref-106" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-106">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup> These include the <a href="/wiki/Endangered_species" title="Endangered species">endangered</a> <a href="/wiki/Bengal_tiger" title="Bengal tiger">Bengal tiger</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Red_panda" title="Red panda">red panda</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Asiatic_elephant" class="mw-redirect" title="Asiatic elephant">Asiatic elephant</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Himalayan_musk_deer" class="mw-redirect" title="Himalayan musk deer">Himalayan musk deer</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Wild_water_buffalo" title="Wild water buffalo">wild water buffalo</a> and the <a href="/wiki/South_Asian_river_dolphin" title="South Asian river dolphin">South Asian river dolphin</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-107" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-107">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> as well as the <a href="/wiki/Critically_endangered" class="mw-redirect" title="Critically endangered">critically endangered</a> <a href="/wiki/Gharial" title="Gharial">gharial</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Bengal_florican" title="Bengal florican">Bengal florican</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-wwf_88-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-wwf-88">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-108" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-108">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup> and the <a href="/wiki/White-rumped_vulture" title="White-rumped vulture">white-rumped vulture</a>, which has become nearly extinct by having ingested the carrion of <a href="/wiki/Diclofenac" title="Diclofenac">diclofenac</a>-treated cattle.<sup id="cite_ref-thtvul_109-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-thtvul-109">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup> The pervasive and ecologically devastating human encroachment of recent decades has critically endangered <a href="/wiki/Wildlife_of_Nepal" title="Wildlife of Nepal">Nepali wildlife</a>. In response, the system of <a href="/wiki/Protected_areas_of_Nepal" class="mw-redirect" title="Protected areas of Nepal">national parks and protected areas</a>, first established in 1973 with the enactment of <i>National Parks and Wildlife Conservation Act 1973</i>,<sup id="cite_ref-110" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-110">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup> was substantially expanded. <i>Vulture restaurants</i><sup id="cite_ref-kindl_89-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-kindl-89">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup> coupled with a ban on veterinary usage of diclofenac has seen a rise in the number of white-rumped vultures.<sup id="cite_ref-111" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-111">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-thtvul_109-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-thtvul-109">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Community_forestry_in_Nepal" title="Community forestry in Nepal">community forestry programme</a> which has seen a third of the country's population directly participate in managing a quarter of the total forested area has helped the local economies while reducing <a href="/wiki/Human-wildlife_conflict" class="mw-redirect" title="Human-wildlife conflict">human-wildlife conflict</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-112" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-112">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-113" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-113">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup> The breeding programmes<sup id="cite_ref-114" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-114">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup> coupled with community-assisted military patrols,<sup id="cite_ref-115" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-115">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> and a crackdown on poaching and smuggling, has seen poaching of critically endangered tigers and elephants as well as <a href="/wiki/Vulnerable_species" title="Vulnerable species">vulnerable</a> rhinos, among others, go down to effectively zero, and their numbers have steadily increased.<sup id="cite_ref-116" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-116">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup> Nepal has ten <a href="/wiki/National_park" title="National park">national parks</a>, three <a href="/wiki/Wildlife_reserves" class="mw-redirect" title="Wildlife reserves">wildlife reserves</a>, one <a href="/wiki/Game_reserve" title="Game reserve">hunting reserve</a>, three <a href="/wiki/Conservation_Area" class="mw-redirect" title="Conservation Area">Conservation Areas</a> and eleven <a href="/wiki/Buffer_zone" title="Buffer zone">buffer zones</a>, covering a total area of 28,959.67&#160;km<sup>2</sup> (11,181.39&#160;sq&#160;mi), or 19.67% of the total land area,<sup id="cite_ref-117" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-117">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup> while ten <a
