<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Language">Language</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Languages_of_Nepal" title="Languages of Nepal">Languages of Nepal</a></div>
<p>Nepal's diverse linguistic heritage stems from three major language groups: <a href="/wiki/Indo-Aryan_languages" title="Indo-Aryan languages">Indo-Aryan</a>, <a href="/wiki/Tibeto-Burman" class="mw-redirect" title="Tibeto-Burman">Tibeto-Burman</a>, and various <a href="/wiki/Indigenous_language" title="Indigenous language">indigenous language</a> isolates. The major languages of Nepal (percent spoken as native language) according to the 2011 census are <a href="/wiki/Nepali_language" title="Nepali language">Nepali</a> (44.6%), <a href="/wiki/Maithili_language" title="Maithili language">Maithili</a> (11.7%), <a href="/wiki/Bhojpuri_language" title="Bhojpuri language">Bhojpuri</a> (6.0%), <a href="/wiki/Tharu_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Tharu language">Tharu</a> (5.8%), <a href="/wiki/Tamang_language" title="Tamang language">Tamang</a> (5.1%), <a href="/wiki/Nepal_Bhasa" class="mw-redirect" title="Nepal Bhasa">Nepal Bhasa</a> (3.2%), <a href="/wiki/Bajjika_dialect" class="mw-redirect" title="Bajjika dialect">Bajjika</a> (3%) and <a href="/wiki/Magar_language" title="Magar language">Magar</a> (3.0%), <a href="/wiki/Doteli_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Doteli language">Doteli</a> (3.0%), <a href="/wiki/Urdu" title="Urdu">Urdu</a> (2.6%), <a href="/wiki/Awadhi" class="mw-redirect" title="Awadhi">Awadhi</a> (1.89%), and <a href="/wiki/Sunwar_language" title="Sunwar language">Sunwar</a>. Nepal is home to at least four indigenous <a href="/wiki/Nepali_Sign_Language" title="Nepali Sign Language">sign languages</a>.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (May 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Descendent of <a href="/wiki/Sanskrit" title="Sanskrit">Sanskrit</a>, <a href="/wiki/Nepali_language" title="Nepali language">Nepali</a> is written in <a href="/wiki/Devanagari" title="Devanagari">Devanagari</a> script. It is the official language and serves as <i>lingua franca</i> among Nepali of different ethnolinguistic groups. The regional languages <a href="/wiki/Maithili_language" title="Maithili language">Maithili</a>, <a href="/wiki/Awadhi_language" title="Awadhi language">Awadhi</a> and Bhojpuri are spoken in the southern Terai region; <a href="/wiki/Urdu" title="Urdu">Urdu</a> is common among <a href="/wiki/Nepali_Muslims" class="mw-redirect" title="Nepali Muslims">Nepali Muslims</a>. <a href="/wiki/Tibetic_languages" title="Tibetic languages">Varieties of Tibetan</a> are spoken in and north of the higher Himalaya where standard literary Tibetan is widely understood by those with religious education. Local dialects in the Terai and hills are mostly unwritten with efforts underway to develop systems for writing many in Devanagari or the Roman alphabet.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (May 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Religion">Religion</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Religion_in_Nepal" title="Religion in Nepal">Religion in Nepal</a></div>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Sadus_at_Pashupatinath_temple.JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b2/Sadus_at_Pashupatinath_temple.JPG/220px-Sadus_at_Pashupatinath_temple.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b2/Sadus_at_Pashupatinath_temple.JPG/330px-Sadus_at_Pashupatinath_temple.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b2/Sadus_at_Pashupatinath_temple.JPG/440px-Sadus_at_Pashupatinath_temple.JPG 2x" data-file-width="4608" data-file-height="3072" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Sadus_at_Pashupatinath_temple.JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Sadhu" title="Sadhu">Sadhus</a> in <a href="/wiki/Pashupatinath_Temple" title="Pashupatinath Temple">Pashupatinath Temple</a></div></div></div>
<p>Nepal is a secular country, as declared by the Constitution of Nepal 2012 (Part 1, Article 4), where secularism 'means religious, cultural freedom, along with the protection of religion, culture handed down from time immemorial (<span title="Hindi-language text"><span lang="hi">सनातन</span></span>)'.<sup id="cite_ref-251" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-251">&#91;241&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-252" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-252">&#91;242&#93;</a></sup> The 2011 census reported that the religion with the largest number of followers in Nepal was <a href="/wiki/Hinduism_in_Nepal" title="Hinduism in Nepal">Hinduism</a> (81.3% of the population), followed by <a href="/wiki/Buddhism_in_Nepal" title="Buddhism in Nepal">Buddhism</a> (9%); the remaining were <a href="/wiki/Islam_in_Nepal" title="Islam in Nepal">Islam</a> (4.4%), <a href="/wiki/Kirat_Mundhum" title="Kirat Mundhum">Kirant</a> (3.1%), <a href="/wiki/Christianity_in_Nepal" title="Christianity in Nepal">Christianity</a> (1.4%) and <i>Prakriti</i> or nature worship (0.5%).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTE2011_National_Census4,_184_253-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTE2011_National_Census4,_184-253">&#91;243&#93;</a></sup> By percentage of population, Nepal has the largest population of Hindus in the world.<sup id="cite_ref-254" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-254">&#91;244&#93;</a></sup> Nepal was officially a Hindu Kingdom until recently, and <a href="/wiki/Shiva" title="Shiva">Shiva</a> was considered the guardian deity of the country.<sup id="cite_ref-255" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-255">&#91;245&#93;</a></sup> Although many government policies throughout history have disregarded or marginalised minority religions, Nepalese societies generally enjoy religious tolerance and harmony among all religions, with only isolated incidents of religiously motivated violence.<sup id="cite_ref-256" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-256">&#91;246&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-257" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-257">&#91;247&#93;</a></sup> Nepal's constitution does not give anyone the right to convert any person to another religion. Nepal also passed a more stringent <a href="/wiki/Anti-conversion_law" title="Anti-conversion law">anti-conversion law</a> on 2017.<sup id="cite_ref-258" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-258">&#91;248&#93;</a></sup> Nepal has the second-largest number of <a href="/wiki/Hindu" class="mw-redirect" title="Hindu">Hindus</a> in the world after India.<sup id="cite_ref-PewDec2012_259-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-PewDec2012-259">&#91;249&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div style="clear:both;"></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Education">Education</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Education_in_Nepal" title="Education in Nepal">Education in Nepal</a></div>
<p>Nepal entered modernity in 1951 with a literacy rate of 5% and about 10,000 students enrolled in 300 schools.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (September 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> By 2017, there were more than seven million students enrolled in 35,601 schools.<sup id="cite_ref-moe17_260-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-moe17-260">&#91;250&#93;</a></sup> The overall literacy rate (for population age 5 years and above) increased from 54.1% in 2001 to 65.9% in 2011.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTE2011_National_Census4_5-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTE2011_National_Census4-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> The net primary enrolment rate reached 97% by 2017,<sup id="cite_ref-undp_261-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-undp-261">&#91;251&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-262" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-262">&#91;252&#93;</a></sup> yet enrolment was less than 60% at the secondary level (grades 9 –12),<sup id="cite_ref-uis_263-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-uis-263">&#91;253&#93;</a></sup> and around 12% at the tertiary level.<sup id="cite_ref-uis_263-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-uis-263">&#91;253&#93;</a></sup> Though there is significant gender disparity in overall literacy rate,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTE2011_National_Census4_5-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTE2011_National_Census4-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> girls have overtaken boys in enrolment to all levels of education.<sup id="cite_ref-uis_263-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-uis-263">&#91;253&#93;</a></sup> Nepal has eleven universities and four independent science academies.<sup id="cite_ref-moe17_260-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-moe17-260">&#91;250&#93;</a></sup> Nepal was ranked 111st in the <a href="/wiki/Global_Innovation_Index" title="Global Innovation Index">Global Innovation Index</a> in 2021, down from 109th in 2019.<sup id="cite_ref-264" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-264">&#91;254&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-265" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-265">&#91;255&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-266" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-266">&#91;256&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-267" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-267">&#91;257&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Lack of proper infrastructures and teaching materials, and a high student-to-teacher ratio, as well as politicisation of school management committees<sup id="cite_ref-268" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-268">&#91;258&#93;</a></sup> and partisan unionisation among both students and teachers,<sup id="cite_ref-269" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-269">&#91;259&#93;</a></sup> present a hurdle to progress. Free basic education is guaranteed in the constitution but the programme lacks funding for effective implementation.<sup id="cite_ref-270" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-270">&#91;260&#93;</a></sup> Government has scholarship programmes for girls and disabled students as well as the children of martyrs, marginalised communities and the poor.<sup id="cite_ref-271" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-271">&#91;261&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-272" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-272">&#91;262&#93;</a></sup> Tens of thousands of Nepali students leave the country every year in search of better education and work, with half of them never returning.<sup id="cite_ref-273" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-273">&#91;263&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-274" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-274">&#91;264&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Health">Health</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Health_in_Nepal" title="Health in Nepal">Health in Nepal</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Life_expectancy_in_Nepal.svg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/aa/Life_expectancy_in_Nepal.svg/220px-Life_expectancy_in_Nepal.svg.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="155" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/aa/Life_expectancy_in_Nepal.svg/330px-Life_expectancy_in_Nepal.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/aa/Life_expectancy_in_Nepal.svg/440px-Life_expectancy_in_Nepal.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="850" data-file-height="600" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Life_expectancy_in_Nepal.svg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Historical development of life expectancy in Nepal</div></div></div>
<p>Health care services in Nepal are provided by both the public and private sectors. Life expectancy at birth is estimated at 71 years as of 2017, 153rd highest in the world,<sup id="cite_ref-275" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-275">&#91;265&#93;</a></sup> up from 54 years in the 1990s and 35 years in 1950.<sup id="cite_ref-ththeal_276-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ththeal-276">&#91;266&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-277" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-277">&#91;267&#93;</a></sup> Two-thirds of all deaths are due to non-communicable diseases; heart disease is the leading cause of death.<sup id="cite_ref-nhrc19_278-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nhrc19-278">&#91;268&#93;</a></sup> While sedentary lifestyle, imbalanced diet and consumption of tobacco and alcohol has contributed in the rise of non-communicable diseases, many lose their life to communicable and treatable diseases caused by poor sanitation and malnutrition due to a lack of education, awareness and access to healthcare services.<sup id="cite_ref-nhrc19_278-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nhrc19-278">&#91;268&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-tkpheal19_279-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-tkpheal19-279">&#91;269&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Nepal has made great progress in maternal and child health. 95% of children have access to iodised salt, and 86% of children aged 6 – 59 months receive Vitamin A prophylaxis.<sup id="cite_ref-unicefhealth_280-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-unicefhealth-280">&#91;270&#93;</a></sup> Stunting, underweight and wasting has been reduced significantly;<sup id="cite_ref-unicefhealth_280-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-unicefhealth-280">&#91;270&#93;</a></sup> malnutrition, at 43% among children under five, is extremely high.<sup id="cite_ref-ntimes19_281-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ntimes19-281">&#91;271&#93;</a></sup> Anemia in women and children increased between 2011 and 2016, reaching 41% and 53% respectively.<sup id="cite_ref-ntimes19_281-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ntimes19-281">&#91;271&#93;</a></sup> Low birth weight is at 27% while breastfeeding is at 65%.<sup id="cite_ref-ntimes19_281-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ntimes19-281">&#91;271&#93;</a></sup> Nepal has reduced maternal mortality rate to 229,<sup id="cite_ref-kpmm_282-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-kpmm-282">&#91;272&#93;</a></sup> from 901 in 1990;<sup id="cite_ref-whomm_283-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-whomm-283">&#91;273&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-kpmm_282-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-kpmm-282">&#91;272&#93;</a></sup> infant mortality is down to 32.2 per thousand live births compared to 139.8 in 1990.<sup id="cite_ref-284" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-284">&#91;274&#93;</a></sup> Contraceptive prevalence rate is 53% but the disparity rate between rural and urban areas is high due to a lack of awareness and easy access.<sup id="cite_ref-285" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-285">&#91;275&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Progress in health is driven by strong government initiative in cooperation with NGOs and INGOs. Public health centres provide 72 essential medicines free of cost. In addition, the public health insurance plan initiated in 2016 which covers health treatments of up to Rs 50,000 for five members of a family, for a premium of Rs 2500 per year, has seen limited success, and is expected to expand.<sup id="cite_ref-286" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-286">&#91;276&#93;</a></sup> By paying stipends for four antenatal visits to health centres and hospitalised delivery, Nepal decreased home-births from 81% in 2006<sup id="cite_ref-whomm_283-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-whomm-283">&#91;273&#93;</a></sup> to 41% in 2016.<sup id="cite_ref-287" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-287">&#91;277&#93;</a></sup> School meal programmes have improved education as well as nutrition metrics among children.<sup id="cite_ref-288" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-288">&#91;278&#93;</a></sup> Toilet building subsidies under the ambitious "one household-one toilet" programme has seen toilet prevalence rate reach 99% in 2019, from just 6% in 1990.<sup id="cite_ref-289" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-289">&#91;279&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Immigrants_and_refugees">Immigrants and refugees</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Demographics_of_Nepal#Foreign_population_in_Nepal" title="Demographics of Nepal">Demographics of Nepal §&#160;Foreign population in Nepal</a></div>
<p>Nepal has a long tradition of accepting immigrants and refugees.<sup id="cite_ref-290" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-290">&#91;280&#93;</a></sup> In modern times, Tibetans and Bhutanese have constituted a majority of refugees in Nepal. Tibetan refugees began arriving in 1959,<sup id="cite_ref-291" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-291">&#91;281&#93;</a></sup> and many more cross into Nepal every year.<sup id="cite_ref-292" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-292">&#91;282&#93;</a></sup> The Bhutanese <a href="/wiki/Lhotshampa" title="Lhotshampa">Lhotsampa</a> refugees began arriving in the 1980s and numbered more than 110,000 by the 2000s. Most of them have been resettled in third countries.<sup id="cite_ref-293" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-293">&#91;283&#93;</a></sup> In late 2018, Nepal had a total of 20,800 confirmed refugees, 64% of them Tibetan and 31% Bhutanese.<sup id="cite_ref-294" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-294">&#91;284&#93;</a></sup> Economic immigrants, and refugees fleeing persecution or war, from neighbouring countries, Africa and the Middle East, termed "urban refugees" because they live in apartments in the cities instead of refugee camps,<sup id="cite_ref-295" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-295">&#91;285&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-296" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-296">&#91;286&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-297" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-297">&#91;287&#93;</a></sup> lack official recognition; the government facilitates their resettlement in third countries.<sup id="cite_ref-298" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-298">&#91;288&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Around 2,000 immigrants, half of them Chinese, applied for a work permit in 2018/19. The government lacks data on Indian immigrants as they do not require permits to live and work in Nepal;<sup id="cite_ref-299" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-299">&#91;289&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Government_of_India" title="Government of India">Government of India</a> puts the number of <a href="/wiki/Non-Resident_Indians" class="mw-redirect" title="Non-Resident Indians">Non-Resident Indians</a> in the country at 600,000.<sup id="cite_ref-mea.gov.in_300-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-mea.gov.in-300">&#91;290&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Culture">Culture</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Culture_of_Nepal" title="Culture of Nepal">Culture of Nepal</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Magar_Culture.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e5/Magar_Culture.jpg/170px-Magar_Culture.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="226" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e5/Magar_Culture.jpg/255px-Magar_Culture.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e5/Magar_Culture.jpg/340px-Magar_Culture.jpg 2x" data-file-width="392" data-file-height="522" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Magar_Culture.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>A <a href="/wiki/Magars" title="Magars">Magar</a> couple in their ethnic dress</div></div></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Society">Society</span></h3>
<p>Traditional Nepali society is sometimes defined by social hierarchy. The <a href="/wiki/Caste_system_in_Nepal" title="Caste system in Nepal">Nepali caste system</a> embodies much of the social stratification and many of the social restrictions found in South Asia. Social classes are defined by more than a hundred <a href="/wiki/Endogamous" class="mw-redirect" title="Endogamous">endogamous</a> hereditary groups, often termed as <i><a href="/wiki/J%C4%81ti" title="Jāti">jātis</a></i>, or "castes". Nepal declared <a href="/wiki/Untouchability" title="Untouchability">untouchability</a> to be illegal in 1963<sup id="cite_ref-301" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-301">&#91;291&#93;</a></sup> and has since enacted other anti-discriminatory laws and social welfare initiatives. At the workplace and educational institutions in urban Nepal, caste-related identification has pretty much lost its importance.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (May 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Family values are important in the Nepali tradition, and multi-generational patriarchal joint families have been the norm in Nepal, though nuclear families are becoming common in urban areas. An overwhelming majority of Nepalis, with or without their consent, have <a href="/wiki/Arranged_marriage_in_the_Indian_subcontinent" title="Arranged marriage in the Indian subcontinent">their marriages arranged</a> by their parents or other family elders. Marriage is thought to be for life, and the divorce rate is extremely low, with less than one in a thousand marriages ending in divorce.<sup id="cite_ref-302" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-302">&#91;292&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Child_marriage" title="Child marriage">Child marriages</a> are common, especially in rural areas; many women wed before reaching 18.<sup id="cite_ref-303" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-303">&#91;293&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Many <a href="/wiki/Public_holidays_in_Nepal" title="Public holidays in Nepal">Nepali festivals</a> are religious in origin. The best known include: <a href="/wiki/Dashain" title="Dashain">Dashain</a>, <a href="/wiki/Tihar_(festival)" title="Tihar (festival)">Tihar</a>, <a href="/wiki/Teej" title="Teej">Teej</a>, <a href="/wiki/Chhath" title="Chhath">Chhath</a>, <a href="/wiki/Maghi" title="Maghi">Maghi</a>, <a href="/wiki/Sakela" title="Sakela">Sakela</a>, <a href="/wiki/Holi" title="Holi">Holi</a>, <a href="/wiki/Eid_ul-Fitr" class="mw-redirect" title="Eid ul-Fitr">Eid ul-Fitr</a>, <a href="/wiki/Christmas" title="Christmas">Christmas</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/Vaisakhi" title="Vaisakhi">Nepali new year</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Symbols">Symbols</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/National_symbols_of_Nepal" title="National symbols of Nepal">National symbols of Nepal</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"/><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1135587883">.mw-parser-output .ib-region-symbols-left{float:left;clear:left;margin:0.5em 1em 0.5em 0}.mw-parser-output .ib-region-symbols-none{float:none;clear:none;margin:0.5em 1em 0.5em 0}.mw-parser-output .ib-region-symbols-center{float:none;clear:none;margin:0.5em auto}.mw-parser-output .ib-region-symbols .infobox-title{font-size:120%}.mw-parser-output .ib-region-symbols .infobox-subheader{font-weight:bold;background-color:#cddeff}.mw-parser-output .ib-region-symbols-island{background-color:#cef2e0}</style><table class="infobox ib-region-symbols"><caption class="infobox-title"><a href="/wiki/National_symbols_of_Nepal" title="National symbols of Nepal">National symbols</a></caption><tbody><tr class="mergedtoprow"><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Emblem</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Emblem_of_Nepal" title="Emblem of Nepal">Emblem of Nepal</a></td></tr><tr class="mergedrow"><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Currency</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Nepalese_rupee" title="Nepalese rupee">Nepalese rupee</a> (रू) (NPR)</td></tr><tr class="mergedrow"><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Mammal</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Cow" class="mw-redirect" title="Cow">Cow</a><sup id="cite_ref-304" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-304">&#91;294&#93;</a></sup></td></tr><tr class="mergedrow"><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Bird</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Himalayan_monal" title="Himalayan monal">Himalayan monal</a></td></tr><tr class="mergedrow"><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Flower</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Rhododendron_arboreum" title="Rhododendron arboreum">Rhododendron arboreum</a><sup id="cite_ref-305" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-305">&#91;295&#93;</a></sup></td></tr><tr class="mergedrow"><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Sport</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Volleyball" title="Volleyball">Volleyball</a><sup id="cite_ref-voll_306-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-voll-306">&#91;296&#93;</a></sup></td></tr><tr class="mergedrow"><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Colour</th><td class="infobox-data"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r981673959">.mw-parser-output .legend{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .legend-color{display:inline-block;min-width:1.25em;height:1.25em;line-height:1.25;margin:1px 0;text-align:center;border:1px solid black;background-color:transparent;color:black}.mw-parser-output .legend-text{}</style><span class="legend-color" style="background-color:crimson; color:white;">&#160;</span> <a href="/wiki/Crimson" title="Crimson">Crimson</a></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>The emblem of Nepal depicts the snowy Himalayas, the forested hills, and the fertile Terai, supported by a wreath of rhododendrons, with the national flag at the crest and in the foreground, a plain white map of Nepal below it, and a man's and woman's right hands joined to signify gender equality. At the bottom is the national motto, a Sanskrit quote of patriotism attributed in Nepali folklore to Lord Rama, written in Devanagari script—"<i>Mother and motherland are greater than heaven</i>".<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (May 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Flag_of_Nepal" title="Flag of Nepal">Nepal's flag</a> is the only national flag in the world that is not rectangular in shape.<sup id="cite_ref-307" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-307">&#91;297&#93;</a></sup> The constitution contains instructions for a <a href="/wiki/Geometric_Construction" class="mw-redirect" title="Geometric Construction">Geometric Construction</a> of the double-pennant flag.<sup id="cite_ref-308" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-308">&#91;298&#93;</a></sup> According to its official description, the crimson in the flag stands for victory in war or courage, and is also the colour of the <a href="/wiki/Rhododendron" title="Rhododendron">rhododendron</a>. The flag's blue border signifies Nepali people's desire for peace. The moon on the flag is a symbol of the peaceful and calm nature of Nepalis, while the sun represents the aggressiveness of Nepali warriors.
</p><p>The president is the symbol of national unity. The <a href="/wiki/Martyrs_of_Nepal" title="Martyrs of Nepal">martyrs</a> are the symbols of patriotism. Commanders of the Anglo-Nepalese war, <a href="/wiki/Amar_Singh_Thapa" title="Amar Singh Thapa">Amar Singh Thapa</a>, <a href="/wiki/Bhakti_Thapa" title="Bhakti Thapa">Bhakti Thapa</a>, and Balbhadra Kunwar are considered war heroes. A special designation of "National hero" has been conferred to 16 people from Nepal's history for their exceptional contributions to the prestige of Nepal. Prithvi Narayan Shah, the founder of modern Nepal, is held in high regard and considered "Father of the Nation" by many.<sup id="cite_ref-309" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-309">&#91;299&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Hutt1991_310-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hutt1991-310">&#91;300&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Art_and_architecture">Art and architecture</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Architecture_of_Nepal" title="Architecture of Nepal">Architecture of Nepal</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1096954695/mw-parser-output/.tmulti"/><div class="thumb tmulti tright"><div class="thumbinner multiimageinner" style="width:329px;max-width:329px"><div class="trow"><div class="tsingle" style="width:172px;max-width:172px"><div class="thumbimage" style="border:none;"><a href="/wiki/File:Nyatapola_Temple.JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/47/Nyatapola_Temple.JPG/170px-Nyatapola_Temple.JPG" decoding="async" width="170" height="227" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/47/Nyatapola_Temple.JPG/255px-Nyatapola_Temple.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/47/Nyatapola_Temple.JPG/340px-Nyatapola_Temple.JPG 2x" data-file-width="2448" data-file-height="3264" /></a></div></div><div class="tsingle" style="width:152px;max-width:152px"><div class="thumbimage" style="border:none;"><a href="/wiki/File:Tusha_Hiti_step-well.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3b/Tusha_Hiti_step-well.jpg/150px-Tusha_Hiti_step-well.jpg" decoding="async" width="150" height="226" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3b/Tusha_Hiti_step-well.jpg/225px-Tusha_Hiti_step-well.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3b/Tusha_Hiti_step-well.jpg/300px-Tusha_Hiti_step-well.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4000" data-file-height="6016" /></a></div></div></div><div class="trow"><div class="tsingle" style="width:327px;max-width:327px"><div class="thumbimage" style="border:none;"><a href="/wiki/File:Shivas_Kinder_-_0219.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5f/Shivas_Kinder_-_0219.jpg/325px-Shivas_Kinder_-_0219.jpg" decoding="async" width="325" height="218" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5f/Shivas_Kinder_-_0219.jpg/488px-Shivas_Kinder_-_0219.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5f/Shivas_Kinder_-_0219.jpg/650px-Shivas_Kinder_-_0219.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3872" data-file-height="2592" /></a></div></div></div><div class="trow" style="display:flex"><div class="thumbcaption">Clockwise from top-left: (a) <a href="/wiki/Nyatapola" class="mw-redirect" title="Nyatapola">Nyatapola</a>, a five storied <a href="/wiki/Pagoda" title="Pagoda">pagoda</a> in Bhaktapur, bejewelled with characteristic stone, metal and wood craftsmanship, has survived at least four major earthquakes.<sup id="cite_ref-unesco_311-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-unesco-311">&#91;301&#93;</a></sup> Pagodas, now an indispensable part of East Asian architecture, are conjectured to have been transmitted to China from Nepal. (b) Nepali stonecraft in a royal water spout (c) A traditional Newar "Ankhijhyal" window in the form of a peacock</div></div></div></div>
<p>The oldest known examples of architecture in Nepal are <a href="/wiki/Stupa" title="Stupa">stupas</a> of early Buddhist constructions in and around <a href="/wiki/Kapilvastu_District" title="Kapilvastu District">Kapilvastu</a> in south-western Nepal, and those constructed by <a href="/wiki/Ashoka" title="Ashoka">Ashoka</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Kathmandu_Valley" title="Kathmandu Valley">Kathmandu Valley</a> <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;250</span> BC. The characteristic architecture associated exclusively with Nepal was developed and refined by Newa artisans of the Kathmandu Valley starting no later than the Lichchhavi period. A <a href="/wiki/Tang_dynasty" title="Tang dynasty">Tang dynasty</a> Chinese travel book, probably based on records from c. 650 AD, describes contemporary Nepali architecture, predominantly built with wood, as rich in artistry, as well as wood and metal sculpture. It describes a magnificent seven-storied pagoda in the middle of a palace, with copper-tiled roofs, its balustrade, grills, columns and beams set about with fine and precious stones, and four golden sculptures of <a href="/wiki/Makara" title="Makara">Makaras</a> in the four corners of the base spouting water from their mouths like a fountain, supplied by copper pipes connected to the runnels at the top of the tower. Later Chinese chronicles describe Nepal's king's palace as an immense structure with many roofs, suggesting that Chinese were not yet familiar with the pagoda architecture, which has now become one of the chief characteristic of Chinese architecture.
</p><p>A typical pagoda temple is built with wood, every piece of it finely carved with geometrical patterns or images of gods, goddesses, mythical beings and beasts. The roofs usually tiled with clay, and sometimes gold plated, diminish in proportion successively until the topmost roof is reached which is itself ensigned by a golden finial. The base is usually composed of rectangular terraces of finely carved stone; the entrance is usually guarded by stone sculptures of conventional figures. Bronze and copper craftsmanship observable in the sculpture of deities and beasts, decorations of doors and windows and the finials of buildings, as well as items of every day use is found to be of equal splendour. The most well-developed of Nepali painting traditions is the <a href="/wiki/Thanka" class="mw-redirect" title="Thanka">thanka</a> or <a href="/wiki/Paubha" title="Paubha">paubha</a> painting tradition of <a href="/wiki/Tibetan_Buddhism" title="Tibetan Buddhism">Tibetan Buddhism</a>, practised in Nepal by the Buddhist monks and Newar artisans. <a href="/wiki/Changu_Narayan_Temple" title="Changu Narayan Temple">Changu Narayan Temple</a>, built c. fourth century AD has probably the finest of Nepali woodcraft; the Kathmandu, Patan and Bhaktapur Durbar Squares are the culmination of Nepali art and architecture, showcasing Nepali wood, metal and stone craftsmanship refined over two millennia.<sup id="cite_ref-landon2_312-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-landon2-312">&#91;302&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Newar_window" title="Newar window">"ankhijhyal" window</a>, that allow a one-way view of the outside world, is an example of unique Nepali woodcraft, found in building structures, domestic and public alike, ancient and modern. Many cultures paint the walls of their homes with regular patterns, figures of gods and beasts and religious symbols; others paint their walls plain, often with clay or <a href="/wiki/Chernozem" title="Chernozem">chernozem</a> contrasted with yellow soil or limestone. The roofs of religious as well as domestic structures project considerably, presumably to provide protection from the sun and the rain. The timber of domestic structures are finely carved as with their religious counterparts.<sup id="cite_ref-landon2_312-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-landon2-312">&#91;302&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Literature_and_the_performing_arts">Literature and the performing arts</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Nepalese_literature" class="mw-redirect" title="Nepalese literature">Nepalese literature</a>, <a href="/wiki/Music_of_Nepal" title="Music of Nepal">Music of Nepal</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Cinema_of_Nepal" title="Cinema of Nepal">Cinema of Nepal</a></div>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Bhanu_Bhakta_Acharya_(1814-1869)_author.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9c/Bhanu_Bhakta_Acharya_%281814-1869%29_author.jpg/170px-Bhanu_Bhakta_Acharya_%281814-1869%29_author.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="283" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9c/Bhanu_Bhakta_Acharya_%281814-1869%29_author.jpg/255px-Bhanu_Bhakta_Acharya_%281814-1869%29_author.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9c/Bhanu_Bhakta_Acharya_%281814-1869%29_author.jpg/340px-Bhanu_Bhakta_Acharya_%281814-1869%29_author.jpg 2x" data-file-width="587" data-file-height="976" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Bhanu_Bhakta_Acharya_(1814-1869)_author.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Bhanubhakta_Acharya" title="Bhanubhakta Acharya">Bhanubhakta Acharya</a>, Nepali writer who translated the ancient Hindu epic <i><a href="/wiki/Ramayana" title="Ramayana">Ramayana</a></i> in the <a href="/wiki/Nepali_language" title="Nepali language">Nepali language</a></div></div></div>
<p>Nepal's literature was closely intertwined with that of the rest of South Asia until its unification into a modern kingdom. Literary works, which were written in Sanskrit by Brahmin priests educated and sometimes also based in Varanasi, included religious texts and other fantasies involving kings, gods and demons.<sup id="cite_ref-bba_313-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bba-313">&#91;303&#93;</a></sup> The oldest extant Nepali language text is dated to the 13th century but except for the epigraphic material, Nepali language literature older than the 17th century haven't been found. Newar literature dates back almost 500 years.<sup id="cite_ref-Hutt1991_310-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hutt1991-310">&#91;300&#93;</a></sup> The modern history of Nepali literature begins with Bhanubhakta Acharya (1814–1868), who for the first time composed major and influential works in Nepali, the language accessible to the masses, most prominently, the <a href="/wiki/Bhanubhakta_Ramayana" title="Bhanubhakta Ramayana">Bhanubhakta Ramayana</a>, a translation of the ancient Hindu epic.<sup id="cite_ref-bba_313-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-bba-313">&#91;303&#93;</a></sup> By the end of the nineteenth century, <a href="/wiki/Motiram_Bhatta" title="Motiram Bhatta">Motiram Bhatta</a> had published print editions of the works of Acharya, and through his efforts, single-handedly popularised and propelled Nepali language literature into modernity.<sup id="cite_ref-Hutt1991_310-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hutt1991-310">&#91;300&#93;</a></sup> By the mid-twentieth century, Nepali literature was no longer limited to the Hindu literary traditions. Influenced by western literary traditions, writers in this period started producing literary works addressing the contemporary social problems,<sup id="cite_ref-314" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-314">&#91;304&#93;</a></sup> while many others continued to enrich Nepali poetic traditions with authentic Nepali poetry. Newar literature also emerged as a premier literary tradition. After the advent of democracy in 1951, Nepali literature flourished. Literary works in many other languages began to be produced. Nepali literature continued to modernise, and in recent years, has been strongly influenced by the post civil-war Nepali experience as well as global literary traditions.<sup id="cite_ref-315" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-315">&#91;305&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-316" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-316">&#91;306&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-317" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-317">&#91;307&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Hutt1991_310-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hutt1991-310">&#91;300&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Maruni" title="Maruni">Maruni</a>, <a href="/wiki/Lakhey" title="Lakhey">Lakhey</a>, <a href="/wiki/Sakela" title="Sakela">Sakela</a>, <a href="/wiki/Kauda" class="mw-redirect" title="Kauda">Kauda</a> and <a href="/wiki/Tamang_Selo" title="Tamang Selo">Tamang Selo</a> are some examples of the traditional Nepali music and dance in the hilly regions of Nepal.
</p><p>Nepali film industry is known as "Kollywood".<sup id="cite_ref-318" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-318">&#91;308&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Nepal Academy is the foremost institution for the promotion of arts and culture in Nepal, established in 1957.<sup id="cite_ref-Hutt1991_310-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hutt1991-310">&#91;300&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Clothing">Clothing</span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Bhoto_jatra.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/42/Bhoto_jatra.jpg/220px-Bhoto_jatra.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="177" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/42/Bhoto_jatra.jpg/330px-Bhoto_jatra.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/42/Bhoto_jatra.jpg/440px-Bhoto_jatra.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2658" data-file-height="2136" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Bhoto_jatra.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>A Nepali man in Daura-Suruwal, coat and Dhaka topi, displays the bhoto during the <a href="/wiki/Bhoto_Jatra" class="mw-redirect" title="Bhoto Jatra">Bhoto Jatra</a> festival.</div></div></div>
<p>The most widely worn traditional dress in Nepal, for both women and men, from ancient times until the advent of modern times, was draped.<sup id="cite_ref-Tarlo1996-26_319-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Tarlo1996-26-319">&#91;309&#93;</a></sup> For women, it eventually took the form of a <a href="/wiki/Sari" title="Sari">sari</a>, a single long piece of cloth, famously six yards long, and of width spanning the lower body.<sup id="cite_ref-Tarlo1996-26_319-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Tarlo1996-26-319">&#91;309&#93;</a></sup> The sari is tied around the waist and knotted at one end, wrapped around the lower body, and then over the shoulder.<sup id="cite_ref-Tarlo1996-26_319-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Tarlo1996-26-319">&#91;309&#93;</a></sup> In its more modern form, it has been used to cover the head, and sometimes the face, as a veil,<sup id="cite_ref-Tarlo1996-26_319-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Tarlo1996-26-319">&#91;309&#93;</a></sup> particularly in the Terai. It has been combined with an underskirt, or the <a href="/wiki/Petticoat#Asian_petticoats" title="Petticoat">petticoat</a>, and tucked in the waistband for more secure fastening. It is worn with a <a href="/wiki/Blouse" title="Blouse">blouse</a>, or <a href="/wiki/Choli" title="Choli">cholo</a>, which serves as the primary upper-body garment, the sari's end, passing over the shoulder, now serving to obscure the upper body's contours, and to cover the midriff.<sup id="cite_ref-Tarlo1996-26_319-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Tarlo1996-26-319">&#91;309&#93;</a></sup> Cholo-sari has become the attire of choice for formal occasions, official environs and festive gatherings. In its more traditional form, as part of traditional dresses and as worn in daily life while performing household chores or labour, it takes the form of a <span title="Nepali (macrolanguage)-language romanization"><i lang="ne-Latn">fariya</i></span> or <span title="Nepali (macrolanguage)-language romanization"><i lang="ne-Latn">gunyu</i></span>, usually shorter than a sari in length as well as breadth, and all of it wrapped around the lower body.
</p><p>For men, a similar but shorter length of cloth, the <a href="/wiki/Dhoti" title="Dhoti">dhoti</a>, has served as a lower-body garment.<sup id="cite_ref-Tarlo1996-26-28_320-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Tarlo1996-26-28-320">&#91;310&#93;</a></sup> It too is tied around the waist and wrapped.<sup id="cite_ref-Tarlo1996-26-28_320-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Tarlo1996-26-28-320">&#91;310&#93;</a></sup> Among the Aryans, it is also wrapped once around each leg before being brought up through the legs to be tucked in at the back. Dhoti or its variants, usually worn over a <a href="/wiki/Kaupinam" title="Kaupinam">langauti</a>, constitute the lower-body garment in the traditional clothing of Tharus, Gurungs and Magars as well as the Madhesi people, among others. Other forms of traditional apparel that involve no stitching or tailoring are <a href="/w/index.php?title=Patuka&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Patuka (page does not exist)">patukas</a> (a length of cloth wrapped tightly over the waist by both sexes as a waistband, a part of most traditional Nepali costumes, usually with a khukuri tucked into it when worn by men), scarves like <span title="Nepali (macrolanguage)-language romanization"><i lang="ne-Latn">pachhyauras</i></span> and <span title="Nepali (macrolanguage)-language romanization"><i lang="ne-Latn">majetros</i></span> and shawls like the newar <i>ga</i> and Tibetan <a href="/wiki/Khata" title="Khata">khata</a>, ghumtos (the wedding veils) and various kinds of <a href="/wiki/Turban" title="Turban">turbans</a> (scarves worn around the head as a part of a tradition, or to keep off the sun or the cold,<sup id="cite_ref-Tarlo1996-26-28_320-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Tarlo1996-26-28-320">&#91;310&#93;</a></sup> called a <a href="/wiki/Pheta" title="Pheta">pheta</a>, pagri or sirpau).
</p><p>Until the beginning of the first millennium AD, the ordinary dress of people in South Asia was entirely unstitched.<sup id="cite_ref-Rahman-Alkazi2002_321-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rahman-Alkazi2002-321">&#91;311&#93;</a></sup> The arrival of the Kushans from <a href="/wiki/Central_Asia" title="Central Asia">Central Asia</a>, <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;48</span> AD, popularised cut and sewn garments in the style of Central Asia.<sup id="cite_ref-Rahman-Alkazi2002_321-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Rahman-Alkazi2002-321">&#91;311&#93;</a></sup> The simplest form of sewn clothing, Bhoto (a rudimentary vest), is a universal unisex clothing for children, and traditionally the only clothing children wear until they come of age and are given adult garb, sometimes in a ceremonial rite of passage, such as the gunyu-choli ceremony for Hindu girls. Men continue to wear bhoto through adulthood. Upper body garment for men is usually a vest such as the <i>bhoto</i>, or a shirt similar to the <i><a href="/wiki/Kurta" title="Kurta">kurta</a></i>, such as <i>daura</i>, a closed-necked double-breasted long shirt with five pleats and eight strings that serve to tie it around the body. <i>Suruwal</i>, simply translated as a pair of trousers, is an alternative to and, more recently, replacement for <i>dhoti</i>, <span title="Nepali (macrolanguage)-language romanization"><i lang="ne-Latn">kachhad</i></span> (Magars) or <span title="Nepali (macrolanguage)-language romanization"><i lang="ne-Latn">lungi</i></span> (Tharus); it is traditionally much wider above the knees but tapers below, to fit tightly at the ankles, and is tied to the waist with a drawstring. Modern cholos worn with sarees are usually half-sleeved and single-breasted, and do not cover the midriff. The traditional one called the chaubandi cholo, like the daura, is full-sleeved, double-breasted with pleats and strings, and extends down to the patuka, covering the midriff.
</p><p>Daura-Suruwal and Gunyu-Cholo were the national dresses for men and women respectively until 2011 when they were removed to eliminate favouritism.<sup id="cite_ref-322" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-322">&#91;312&#93;</a></sup> Traditional dresses of many pahari ethnic groups are Daura-Suruwal or similar, with patuka, a dhaka topi and a coat for men, and Gunyu-cholo or similar, with patuka and sometimes a scarf for women. For many other groups, men's traditional dresses consist of a shirt or a vest, paired with a <i>dhoti</i>, <span title="Nepali (macrolanguage)-language romanization"><i lang="ne-Latn">kachhad</i></span> or <span title="Nepali (macrolanguage)-language romanization"><i lang="ne-Latn">lungi</i></span>. In the high Himalayas, the traditional dresses are largely influenced by Tibetan culture. Sherpa women wear the <a href="/wiki/Chuba" title="Chuba">chuba</a> with the <a href="/w/index.php?title=Pangi_(apron)&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Pangi (apron) (page does not exist)">pangi</a> apron, while Sherpa men wear shirts with stiff high collar and long sleeves called tetung under the chuba. Tibetan Xamo Gyaise hats of the Sherpas, <a href="/wiki/Dhaka_topi" title="Dhaka topi">dhaka topi</a> of pahari men and tamang round caps are among the more distinctive headwears.
</p><p>Married Hindu women wear <span title="Nepali (macrolanguage)-language romanization"><i lang="ne-Latn">tika</i></span>, <span title="Nepali (macrolanguage)-language romanization"><i lang="ne-Latn">sindur</i></span>, <span title="Nepali (macrolanguage)-language romanization"><i lang="ne-Latn">pote</i></span> and red bangles. Jewellery of gold and silver, and sometimes precious stones, are common. Gold jewellery includes <span title="Nepali (macrolanguage)-language romanization"><i lang="ne-Latn">mangalsutras</i></span> and <span title="Nepali (macrolanguage)-language romanization"><i lang="ne-Latn">tilaharis</i></span> worn with the <span title="Nepali (macrolanguage)-language romanization"><i lang="ne-Latn">pote</i></span> by the Hindus, <span title="Nepali (macrolanguage)-language romanization"><i lang="ne-Latn">samyafung</i></span> (a huge gold flower worn on the head) and Nessey (huge flattened gold earrings) worn by the Limbus, and <span title="Nepali (macrolanguage)-language romanization"><i lang="ne-Latn">sirphuli</i></span>, <span title="Nepali (macrolanguage)-language romanization"><i lang="ne-Latn">sirbandhi</i></span> and <span title="Nepali (macrolanguage)-language romanization"><i lang="ne-Latn">chandra</i></span> worn by the Magars. Tharu women can wear as much as six kilograms of silver in jewellery, which includes <span title="Nepali (macrolanguage)-language romanization"><i lang="ne-Latn">mangiya</i></span> worn on the head, <span title="Nepali (macrolanguage)-language romanization"><i lang="ne-Latn">tikuli</i></span> the forehead, and <span title="Nepali (macrolanguage)-language romanization"><i lang="ne-Latn">kanseri</i></span> and <span title="Nepali (macrolanguage)-language romanization"><i lang="ne-Latn">tikahamala</i></span> around the neck.<sup id="cite_ref-323" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-323">&#91;313&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the last 50 years, fashions have changed a great deal in Nepal. Increasingly, in urban settings, the sari is no longer the apparel of everyday wear, transformed instead into one for formal occasions. The traditional kurta suruwal is rarely worn by younger women, who increasingly favour jeans. The <i>dhoti</i> has largely been reduced to the <a href="/wiki/Liturgical" class="mw-redirect" title="Liturgical">liturgical</a> <a href="/wiki/Vestment" title="Vestment">vestment</a> of shamans and Hindu priests.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Cuisine">Cuisine</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Nepalese_cuisine" title="Nepalese cuisine">Nepalese cuisine</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Nepali_dal-bhat-tarkari.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/60/Nepali_dal-bhat-tarkari.jpg/170px-Nepali_dal-bhat-tarkari.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="128" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/60/Nepali_dal-bhat-tarkari.jpg/255px-Nepali_dal-bhat-tarkari.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/60/Nepali_dal-bhat-tarkari.jpg/340px-Nepali_dal-bhat-tarkari.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3264" data-file-height="2448" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Nepali_dal-bhat-tarkari.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>A dal-bhat thali with boiled rice, lentil soup, fried leafy greens, vegetable curry, yoghurt, papad and vegetable salad</div></div></div>
<p>Nepali cuisine consists of a wide variety of regional and traditional cuisines. Given the range of diversity in soil type, climate, culture, ethnic groups, and occupations, these cuisines vary substantially from each other, using locally available spices, herbs, vegetables, and fruit.<sup id="cite_ref-tkpcrop_324-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-tkpcrop-324">&#91;314&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Columbian_exchange" title="Columbian exchange">Columbian exchange</a> had brought the potato, the tomato, maize, peanuts, cashew nuts, pineapples, guavas, and most notably, <a href="/wiki/Chilli_peppers" class="mw-redirect" title="Chilli peppers">chilli peppers</a>, to South Asia. Each became staples of use.<sup id="cite_ref-325" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-325">&#91;315&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Cereal" title="Cereal">cereals</a> grown in Nepal, their choice, times, and regions of planting, correspond strongly to the timing of Nepal's monsoons,<sup id="cite_ref-sen-colleen-p13_326-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-sen-colleen-p13-326">&#91;316&#93;</a></sup> and the variations in altitude. Rice and wheat are mostly cultivated in the terai plains and well-irrigated valleys, and maize, millet, barley and buckwheat in the lesser fertile and drier hills.<sup id="cite_ref-tkpcrop_324-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-tkpcrop-324">&#91;314&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-pathak_327-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-pathak-327">&#91;317&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The foundation of a typical Nepali meal is a cereal cooked in plain fashion, and complemented with flavourful savoury dishes.<sup id="cite_ref-Davidson2014-p409_328-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Davidson2014-p409-328">&#91;318&#93;</a></sup> The latter includes <a href="/wiki/Lentil" title="Lentil">lentils</a>, <a href="/wiki/Pulses" class="mw-redirect" title="Pulses">pulses</a> and vegetables spiced commonly with <a href="/wiki/Ginger_root" class="mw-redirect" title="Ginger root">ginger</a> and <a href="/wiki/Garlic" title="Garlic">garlic</a>, but also more discerningly with a combination of spices that may include <a href="/wiki/Coriander" title="Coriander">coriander</a>, <a href="/wiki/Cumin" title="Cumin">cumin</a>, <a href="/wiki/Turmeric" title="Turmeric">turmeric</a>, <a href="/wiki/Cinnamon" title="Cinnamon">cinnamon</a>, <a href="/wiki/Cardamon" class="mw-redirect" title="Cardamon">cardamon</a>, <a href="/wiki/Jimbu" title="Jimbu">jimbu</a> and others as informed by culinary conventions.<sup id="cite_ref-Davidson2014-p409_328-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Davidson2014-p409-328">&#91;318&#93;</a></sup> In an actual meal, this mental representation takes the form of a platter, or <a href="/wiki/Thali" title="Thali">thali</a>, with a central place for the cooked cereal, peripheral ones, often in small bowls, for the flavourful accompaniments, and the simultaneous, rather than piecemeal, ingestion of the two in each act of eating, whether by actual mixing—for example of rice and lentils—or in the folding of one—such as bread—around the other, such as cooked vegetables.<sup id="cite_ref-Davidson2014-p409_328-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Davidson2014-p409-328">&#91;318&#93;</a></sup> <i>Dal-bhat</i>, centred around steamed rice is the most common example.<sup id="cite_ref-cnnfood_329-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cnnfood-329">&#91;319&#93;</a></sup> as well as dairy and sometimes meat, is the most common and prominent example. The unleavened flat bread made from wheat flour called <a href="/wiki/Chapati" title="Chapati">chapati</a> occasionally replaces the steamed rice, particularly in the Terai, while Dhindo, prepared by boiling corn, millet or buckwheat flour in water, continuously stirring and adding flour until thick, almost solid consistency is reached, is the main substitute in the hills and mountains. Tsampa, flour made from roasted barley or naked barley, is the main staple in the high himalayas. Throughout Nepal, fermented, then sun-dried, leafy greens called <i>Gundruk</i>, are both a delicacy and a vital substitute for fresh vegetables in the winter.<sup id="cite_ref-pathak_327-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-pathak-327">&#91;317&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Plateful_of_Momo_in_Nepal.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6c/Plateful_of_Momo_in_Nepal.jpg/220px-Plateful_of_Momo_in_Nepal.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6c/Plateful_of_Momo_in_Nepal.jpg/330px-Plateful_of_Momo_in_Nepal.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6c/Plateful_of_Momo_in_Nepal.jpg/440px-Plateful_of_Momo_in_Nepal.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1024" data-file-height="768" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Plateful_of_Momo_in_Nepal.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Momo dumplings with chutney</div></div></div>
<p>A notable feature of Nepali food is the existence of a number of distinctive vegetarian cuisines, each a feature of the geographical and cultural histories of its adherents.<sup id="cite_ref-Davidson2014-p410_330-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Davidson2014-p410-330">&#91;320&#93;</a></sup> The appearance of <i><a href="/wiki/Ahimsa" title="Ahimsa">ahimsa</a></i>, or the avoidance of violence toward all forms of life in many religious orders early in South Asian history, especially <a href="/wiki/Upanishads" title="Upanishads">Upanishadic Hinduism</a>, <a href="/wiki/Buddhism" title="Buddhism">Buddhism</a> and <a href="/wiki/Jainism" title="Jainism">Jainism</a>, is thought to have been a notable factor in the prevalence of vegetarianism among a segment of Nepal's Hindu and Buddhist populations, as well as among Jains.<sup id="cite_ref-Davidson2014-p410_330-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Davidson2014-p410-330">&#91;320&#93;</a></sup> Among these groups, strong discomfort is felt at thoughts of eating meat.<sup id="cite_ref-SahakianSaloma2016-50_331-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SahakianSaloma2016-50-331">&#91;321&#93;</a></sup> Though per capita meat consumption is low in Nepal, the proportion of vegetarianism is not high as in India, due to the prevalence of <a href="/wiki/Shaktism" title="Shaktism">Shaktism</a>, of which animal sacrifice is a prominent feature.<sup id="cite_ref-332" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-332">&#91;322&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Samaybaji.JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/58/Samaybaji.JPG/170px-Samaybaji.JPG" decoding="async" width="170" height="170" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/58/Samaybaji.JPG/255px-Samaybaji.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/58/Samaybaji.JPG/340px-Samaybaji.JPG 2x" data-file-width="960" data-file-height="960" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Samaybaji.JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Samayabaji (Newar cuisine)</div></div></div>
<p>Nepali cuisines possess their own distinctive qualities to distinguish these hybrid cuisines from both their northern and southern neighbours.<sup id="cite_ref-333" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-333">&#91;323&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-cnnfood_329-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cnnfood-329">&#91;319&#93;</a></sup> Nepali cuisines, with generally tomato-based, leaner curries, are lighter than their cream-based Indian counterparts, and Nepali momo dumplings are heavily spiced compared to their northern counterparts.<sup id="cite_ref-cnnfood_329-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cnnfood-329">&#91;319&#93;</a></sup> Newar cuisine, one of the richest and most influential in Nepal, is more elaborate and diverse than most, as Newar culture developed in the highly fertile and prosperous Kathmandu valley.<sup id="cite_ref-tkpcrop_324-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-tkpcrop-324">&#91;314&#93;</a></sup> A typical Newar cuisine can comprise more than a dozen dishes of cereals, meat, vegetable curries, chutneys and pickles. Kwanti (sprouted beans soup), chhwela (ground beef), <a href="/w/index.php?title=Chat%C4%81nmari&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Chatānmari (page does not exist)">chatamari</a> (rice flour crepe), bara (fried lentil cake), kachila (marinated raw minced beef), samaybaji (centred around flattened rice), <span title="Nepali (macrolanguage)-language romanization"><i lang="ne-Latn">lakhaamari</i></span> and <span title="Nepali (macrolanguage)-language romanization"><i lang="ne-Latn">yomuri</i></span> are among the more widely recognised.<sup id="cite_ref-cnnfood_329-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cnnfood-329">&#91;319&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-334" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-334">&#91;324&#93;</a></sup> Juju dhau, a sweet yoghurt originating in Bhaktapur, is also famous.<sup id="cite_ref-cnnfood_329-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cnnfood-329">&#91;319&#93;</a></sup> Thakali cuisine is another well-known food tradition which seamlessly melds the Tibetan and the Indian with variety in ingredients, especially the herbs and spices.<sup id="cite_ref-tkpcrop_324-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-tkpcrop-324">&#91;314&#93;</a></sup> In the Terai, <a href="/wiki/Bagiya" title="Bagiya">Bagiya</a> is a rice flour dumpling with sweets inside, popular among the Tharu and Maithil people. Various communities in the Terai make <span title="Nepali (macrolanguage)-language romanization"><i lang="ne-Latn">sidhara</i></span> (sun-dried small fish mixed with <a href="/wiki/Taro" title="Taro">taro</a> leaves) and biriya (lentil paste mixed with taro leaves) to stock for the monsoon floods.<sup id="cite_ref-tkpcrop_324-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-tkpcrop-324">&#91;314&#93;</a></sup> <span title="Nepali (macrolanguage)-language romanization"><i lang="ne-Latn">Selroti</i></span>, <span title="Nepali (macrolanguage)-language romanization"><i lang="ne-Latn">kasaar</i></span>, <span title="Nepali (macrolanguage)-language romanization"><i lang="ne-Latn">fini</i></span> and <span title="Nepali (macrolanguage)-language romanization"><i lang="ne-Latn">chaku</i></span> are among the sweet delicacies. Rice pulau or sweet rice porridge called <span title="Nepali (macrolanguage)-language romanization"><i lang="ne-Latn">kheer</i></span> are usually the main dish in feasts.<sup id="cite_ref-pathak_327-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-pathak-327">&#91;317&#93;</a></sup> Tea and buttermilk (fermented milk leftover from churning butter from yoghurt) are common non-alcoholic drinks. Almost all janajati communities have their own traditional methods of brewing alcohol. Raksi (traditional distilled alcohol), jaand (rice beer), tongba (millet beer) and chyaang are the most well-known.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Sports_and_recreation">Sports and recreation</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Sports_in_Nepal" title="Sports in Nepal">Sports in Nepal</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Five-stone_in_Nepal.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4e/Five-stone_in_Nepal.jpg/220px-Five-stone_in_Nepal.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4e/Five-stone_in_Nepal.jpg/330px-Five-stone_in_Nepal.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4e/Five-stone_in_Nepal.jpg/440px-Five-stone_in_Nepal.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4000" data-file-height="3000" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Five-stone_in_Nepal.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Nepali children playing a variant of knucklebones, with pebbles</div></div></div>
<p>Nepali indigenous sports, like <a href="/wiki/Dandi_biyo" title="Dandi biyo">dandi biyo</a> and <a href="/wiki/Kabaddi" title="Kabaddi">kabaddi</a> which were considered the unofficial national sports until recently,<sup id="cite_ref-voll_306-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-voll-306">&#91;296&#93;</a></sup> are still popular in rural areas.<sup id="cite_ref-Himalayan_Times_More_than_child&#39;s_play_335-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Himalayan_Times_More_than_child&#39;s_play-335">&#91;325&#93;</a></sup> Despite efforts, standardisation and development of dandi biyo has not been achieved,<sup id="cite_ref-Online_Khabar_national_sport_336-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Online_Khabar_national_sport-336">&#91;326&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-337" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-337">&#91;327&#93;</a></sup> while Kabaddi, as a professional sport, is still in its infancy in Nepal.<sup id="cite_ref-338" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-338">&#91;328&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Bagh-Chal" title="Bagh-Chal">Bagh-Chal</a>, an ancient <a href="/wiki/Board_games" class="mw-redirect" title="Board games">board game</a> that's thought to have originated in Nepal, can be played on chalk-drawn boards, with pebbles, and is still popular today.<sup id="cite_ref-339" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-339">&#91;329&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-340" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-340">&#91;330&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Ludo_(board_game)" class="mw-redirect" title="Ludo (board game)">Ludo</a>, <a href="/wiki/Snakes_and_ladders" title="Snakes and ladders">snakes and ladders</a> and <a href="/wiki/Carrom" title="Carrom">carrom</a> are popular pastimes.<sup id="cite_ref-341" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-341">&#91;331&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Chess" title="Chess">Chess</a> is also played.<sup id="cite_ref-Himalayan_Times_More_than_child&#39;s_play_335-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Himalayan_Times_More_than_child&#39;s_play-335">&#91;325&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Volleyball" title="Volleyball">Volleyball</a> was declared as the national sport of Nepal in 2017.<sup id="cite_ref-voll_306-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-voll-306">&#91;296&#93;</a></sup> Popular children's games include versions of <a href="/wiki/Tag_(game)" title="Tag (game)">tag</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-Himalayan_Times_More_than_child&#39;s_play_335-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Himalayan_Times_More_than_child&#39;s_play-335">&#91;325&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Knucklebones" title="Knucklebones">knucklebones</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-Himalayan_Times_More_than_child&#39;s_play_335-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Himalayan_Times_More_than_child&#39;s_play-335">&#91;325&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Hopscotch" title="Hopscotch">hopscotch</a>, <a href="/wiki/Duck,_duck,_goose#Rumaal_Chor" title="Duck, duck, goose">Duck, duck, goose</a><sup id="cite_ref-Himalayan_Times_More_than_child&#39;s_play_335-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Himalayan_Times_More_than_child&#39;s_play-335">&#91;325&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Seven_stones" title="Seven stones">lagori</a>, while <a href="/wiki/Marbles" class="mw-redirect" title="Marbles">marbles</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-Himalayan_Times_More_than_child&#39;s_play_335-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Himalayan_Times_More_than_child&#39;s_play-335">&#91;325&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Top" title="Top">top</a>, <a href="/wiki/Hoop_rolling" title="Hoop rolling">hoop rolling</a> and <a href="/wiki/Gully_cricket" class="mw-redirect" title="Gully cricket">gully cricket</a> are also popular among boys. <a href="/wiki/Rubber_band" title="Rubber band">Rubber bands</a>, or <a href="/wiki/Ranger_band" class="mw-redirect" title="Ranger band">ranger bands</a> cut from tubes in <a href="/wiki/Bicycle" title="Bicycle">bike</a> tyres, make a multi-purpose sporting equipment for Nepali children, which may be bunched or chained together, and used to play <a href="/wiki/Dodgeball" title="Dodgeball">dodgeball</a>, <a href="/wiki/Cat%27s_cradle" title="Cat&#39;s cradle">cat's cradle</a>, <a href="/wiki/Jianzi" title="Jianzi">jianzi</a><sup id="cite_ref-Himalayan_Times_More_than_child&#39;s_play_335-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Himalayan_Times_More_than_child&#39;s_play-335">&#91;325&#93;</a></sup> and a variety of <a href="/wiki/Skipping_rope" title="Skipping rope">skipping rope</a> games.<sup id="cite_ref-Himalayan_Times_More_than_child&#39;s_play_335-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Himalayan_Times_More_than_child&#39;s_play-335">&#91;325&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Nepali_Fans.JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/51/Nepali_Fans.JPG/220px-Nepali_Fans.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/51/Nepali_Fans.JPG/330px-Nepali_Fans.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/51/Nepali_Fans.JPG/440px-Nepali_Fans.JPG 2x" data-file-width="4272" data-file-height="2848" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Nepali_Fans.JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Nepali cricket fans are renowned for their exceptionally enthusiastic support of their national team.<sup id="cite_ref-342" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-342">&#91;332&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-343" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-343">&#91;333&#93;</a></sup></div></div></div>
<p><a href="/wiki/Association_football" title="Association football">Football</a> and <a href="/wiki/Cricket" title="Cricket">cricket</a> are popular professional sports.<sup id="cite_ref-344" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-344">&#91;334&#93;</a></sup> Nepal is competitive in football in the South Asia region but has never won the <a href="/wiki/SAFF" class="mw-redirect" title="SAFF">SAFF championships</a>, the regional tournament.<sup id="cite_ref-345" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-345">&#91;335&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-346" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-346">&#91;336&#93;</a></sup> It usually ranks in the bottom quarter in the <a href="/wiki/FIFA_World_Rankings" class="mw-redirect" title="FIFA World Rankings">FIFA World Rankings</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-347" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-347">&#91;337&#93;</a></sup> Nepal has had success in cricket and holds the elite <a href="/wiki/List_of_countries_with_ODI_status" class="mw-redirect" title="List of countries with ODI status">ODI status</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-348" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-348">&#91;338&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-349" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-349">&#91;339&#93;</a></sup> consistently ranking in the Top 20 in the <a href="/wiki/International_Cricket_Council" title="International Cricket Council">ICC</a> <a href="/wiki/ICC_ODI_Championship" class="mw-redirect" title="ICC ODI Championship">ODI</a> and <a href="/wiki/ICC_T20I_Championship" class="mw-redirect" title="ICC T20I Championship">T20I rankings</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-350" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-350">&#91;340&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-351" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-351">&#91;341&#93;</a></sup> Nepal has had some success in <a href="/wiki/Athletics_(sport)" class="mw-redirect" title="Athletics (sport)">athletics</a> and <a href="/wiki/Martial_arts" title="Martial arts">martial arts</a>, having won many medals at the <a href="/wiki/South_Asian_Games" title="South Asian Games">South Asian Games</a> and some at the <a href="/wiki/Asian_games" class="mw-redirect" title="Asian games">Asian games</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-trn_352-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-trn-352">&#91;342&#93;</a></sup> Nepal has never won an <a href="/wiki/Olympic_games" class="mw-redirect" title="Olympic games">olympic</a> medal.<sup id="cite_ref-gauri_353-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-gauri-353">&#91;343&#93;</a></sup> Sports like <a href="/wiki/Basketball" title="Basketball">basketball</a>, volleyball, <a href="/wiki/Futsal" title="Futsal">futsal</a>, <a href="/wiki/Wrestling" title="Wrestling">wrestling</a>, competitive bodybuilding<sup id="cite_ref-gauri_353-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-gauri-353">&#91;343&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-354" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-354">&#91;344&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Badminton" title="Badminton">badminton</a> are also gaining in popularity.<sup id="cite_ref-Himalayan_Times_More_than_child&#39;s_play_335-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Himalayan_Times_More_than_child&#39;s_play-335">&#91;325&#93;</a></sup> Women in football, cricket, athletics, martial arts, badminton and swimming have found some success.<sup id="cite_ref-award_355-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-award-355">&#91;345&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-gauri_353-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-gauri-353">&#91;343&#93;</a></sup> Nepal also fields players and national teams in several tournaments for <a href="/wiki/Disability" title="Disability">disabled</a> individuals, most notably in men's<sup id="cite_ref-menb_356-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-menb-356">&#91;346&#93;</a></sup> as well as women's blind cricket.<sup id="cite_ref-wmenb_357-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-wmenb-357">&#91;347&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The only international stadium in the country is the multi-purpose <a href="/wiki/Dasharath_Rangasala" title="Dasharath Rangasala">Dasarath Stadium</a> where the <a href="/wiki/Nepal_national_football_team" title="Nepal national football team">men</a> and <a href="/wiki/Nepal_women%27s_national_football_team" title="Nepal women&#39;s national football team">women national football teams</a> play their home matches.<sup id="cite_ref-dash_358-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dash-358">&#91;348&#93;</a></sup> Since the formation of the <a href="/wiki/Nepal_national_cricket_team" title="Nepal national cricket team">national team</a>, Nepal has played its home matches of cricket at <a href="/wiki/Tribhuvan_University_International_Cricket_Ground" title="Tribhuvan University International Cricket Ground">Tribhuvan University International Cricket Ground</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-cricktarchv_359-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cricktarchv-359">&#91;349&#93;</a></sup> Nepal police, Armed police force and Nepal army are the most prolific producers of national players, and aspiring players are known to join armed forces, for the better sporting opportunities they can provide.<sup id="cite_ref-360" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-360">&#91;350&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-361" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-361">&#91;351&#93;</a></sup> Nepali sports is hindered by a lack of infrastructure,<sup id="cite_ref-362" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-362">&#91;352&#93;</a></sup> funding,<sup id="cite_ref-menb_356-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-menb-356">&#91;346&#93;</a></sup> corruption, nepotism and political interference.<sup id="cite_ref-trn_352-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-trn-352">&#91;342&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-363" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-363">&#91;353&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-364" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-364">&#91;354&#93;</a></sup> Very few players are able to make a living as professional sportspeople.<sup id="cite_ref-dash_358-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dash-358">&#91;348&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-365" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-365">&#91;355&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
